Dosage Form(s) (Route(s) Injection (for intravenous use)
of administration)
Dosing Regimen Doses of 3 mg/kg/day (up to 150 mg/day) on Days 1, 3, and 5 of
each treatment cycle. Cycles repeated every 4 weeks until
complete response or partial response, followed by 5 additional

cycles every 4 weeks. Subsequent cycles may be repeated every
8 weeks until disease progression or unacceptable toxicity.
Applicant Proposed DANYELZA is indicated or the treatment of refractory or
(b) (4)
Indication(s)/Population(s) relapsed high-risk neuroblastoma in bone
or bone marrow
in combination with GM-CSF
Recommendation on Accelerated Approval
Regulatory Action
Recommended DANYELZA is a GD2-binding monoclonal antibody indicated, in
Indication(s)/Population(s) combination with granulocyte-macrophage colony-stimulating
(if applicable) factor (GM-CSF), for the treatment of pediatric patients 1 year
of age and older and adult patients with relapsed or refractory
high-risk neuroblastoma in the bone or bone marrow who have
demonstrated a partial response, minor response, or stable
disease to prior therapy.

1 Executive Summary
Product Introduction
On March 31, 2020, Y-mAbs Therapeutics, Inc. (Y-mAbs) submitted original Biologics License
Application (BLA) under Section 351(a) of the Public Health Service Act seeking approval of
naxitamab-gqgk (Danyelza) for the treatment of patients with refractory or relapsed high-risk
neuroblastoma in bone or bone marrow in combination with granulocyte-macrophage colonystimulating factor (GM-CSF).
Naxitamab-gqgk (naxitamab, hu3F8-IgG1; will be referred to as naxitamab for the remainder of
this document) is a humanized IgG1 monoclonal antibody directed against the glycolipid
disialoganglioside (GD2), which is a member of the b-series gangliosides and an adhesion
molecule widely expressed on the cell membrane in neuroectoderm-derived tumors; the
established pharmacological class is GD2-binding monoclonal antibody. In vitro, naxitamab was
shown to bind to cell surface GD2 and induce complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC) and
antibody dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC).Naxitamab contains human framework
regions and murine complementarity-determining regions. Naxitamab is produced from a
Chinese hamster ovary cell line and has an approximate molecular weight of 144 kDa without
glycosylation. Naxitamab injection is supplied as a sterile, preservative-free, clear to slightly
opalescent and colorless to slightly yellow solution for intravenous infusion. Each single-dose
vial contains 40 mg of naxitamab in 10 mL of solution. Each mL of solution contains 4 mg of
naxitamab and citric acid anhydrous (0.71 mg), poloxamer 188 (1.5 mg), sodium chloride (7.01
mg), sodium citrate (6.3 mg), and Water for Injection, USP. The pH is approximately 5.7.
Although the two trials supporting efficacy and safety in this application used different
manufacturing processes, OBP determined that the products used in each trial were generally
comparable. Naxitamab is a new molecular entity and has not been previously marketed in the
U.S. or approved for any indication by FDA or globally.

Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
In the opinion of the review team, the submitted evidence meets the statutory evidentiary
standard for accelerated approval. The recommendation for accelerated approval is based on
the results from two single-arm, open-label trials: Trial 201 (NCT 03363373) and Trial 12-230
(NCT 01757626).
Both trials enrolled patients with relapsed or refractory high risk neuroblastoma in the bone or
bone marrow who had demonstrated a response of minor response, partial response, or stable
disease to prior therapy. Patients received naxitamab 9 mg/kg/cycle administered as three
18

and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

{DANYELZA, naxitamab-gqgk}

separate intravenous infusions of 3 mg/kg (on Days 1, 3 and 5) in the first week of each cycle.
Patients received GM-CSF subcutaneously at 250 µg/m2/day on Days -4 to 0 and at 500
µg/m2/day on Days 1 to 5. The primary efficacy population included patients with relapsed or
refractory disease in the bone or bone marrow who had demonstrated a response of stable
disease or better in response to prior therapy, had evaluable disease per blinded independent
central review according to the revised International Neuroblastoma Response Criteria (INRC),
and who received at least one dose of naxitamab. The primary efficacy population was
comprised of 22 patients in Trial 201 and 39 patients in Trial 12-230.
The analysis of efficacy demonstrated a clinically meaningful overall response rate (ORR) per
independent review with sufficient duration to represent a clinically meaningful treatment
effect in pediatric patients with relapsed and refractory neuroblastoma with disease in the
bone or bone marrow. Patients in both trials were permitted to receive radiation in certain
circumstances, although assessment of response excluded sites that received radiation.
Patients for whom radiation was administered to the only site of evaluable disease, which
applied only to patients in Trial 12-230, were excluded from the efficacy analysis. Patients with
a best response of progressive disease to initial therapy or therapy at relapse were excluded
from Trial 201 and from the efficacy analysis population in Trial 12-230. Therefore, the
indication the review team proposes for approval also excludes these patients.
In Trial 201 (n=22), the confirmed ORR in patients with metastatic, relapsed or refractory highrisk neuroblastoma in the bone or bone marrow who demonstrated a response of stable
disease, partial response, or minor response to prior therapy per blinded, independent review
was 45% (95% CI: 24%, 68%), with 30% of the 10 responders having observed duration of
response (DOR) of at least 6 months. The primary efficacy analysis in Trial 201 included 4
patients with prior anti-GD2 treatment; one (25%) of these patients demonstrated a response.
In Trial 12-230 (n=38), the confirmed ORR per blinded, independent review according to the
revised INRC was 34% (95% CI: 20%, 51%), with 23% of the 13 responders having an observed
DOR of 6 months. The primary efficacy analysis in Trial 12-230 included 22 patients with prior
anti-GD2 treatment; 4 (18%) of these patients demonstrated a response (95% CI 5%, 40%).
Given the limited number of patients with prior anti-GD2 exposure, the exploratory analyses of
ORR in this subpopulation should be interpreted with caution; however, the results suggest that
naxitamab has clinical activity in patients who received prior anti-GD2 antibody therapy. The
difference in the point estimates of ORR between the two trials may be explained by the higher
percentage of relapsed patients, who are more heavily pre-treated, in Trial 12-230 (55% vs. 36%
in Trial 201).

Assessment of DOR was limited by administration of additional anticancer therapies
subsequent to achieving a complete or partial response in patients in both trials. Neither study
was designed to include scheduled assessments of response after completion of treatment with
naxitamab, and Trial 201 is currently ongoing. Additional clinical data from Trial 201 may
provide additional data to inform durability of responses.
Given the rarity of relapsed or refractory high-risk neuroblastoma and the lack of an approved
or standard treatment, a randomized trial is not likely to be feasible in this population. The
review team considers that the observed ORR in the two trials, along with the DOR, in patients
treated with naxitamab in combination with GM-CSF sufficient to provide substantial evidence
of the effectiveness of naxitamab in the rare population of patients with relapsed or refractory
neuroblastoma who have disease in the bone or bone marrow. In vitro data demonstrating
ADCC with the monoclonal antibody mu3F8 (a previously studied, murine form of naxitamab)
was provided to support the contribution of effect of GM-CSF. Dose-dependent ADCC was
demonstrated with m3F8, while adding GM-CSF resulted in a reduction in concentration of
mu3F8 required to mediate ADCC. Data from additional patients in this patient population,
including ORR and DOR obtained from a broad representation of clinical sites, may be required
to confirm clinical benefit.

Benefit-Risk Assessment (BRA)

Benefit-Risk Summary and Assessment

Efficacy: The effectiveness of naxitamab for the treatment of relapsed or refractory high-risk neuroblastoma with bone or bone marrow
involvement was established based on data from 38 patients in Trial-230 and 22 patients in Trial 201. Both trials enrolled patients 1 year of age
and older with primary refractory disease or incomplete response to salvage treatment in the bone or bone marrow. In Trial 201, the
confirmed ORR per blinded, independent review was 45% (95% CI: 24%, 68%), with 30% of the 10 responders having an observed DOR of at
least 6 months. The primary efficacy analysis in Trial 201 included 4 patients with prior anti-GD2 treatment, and 1 (25%) of these patients
demonstrated a response. In Trial 12-230, the confirmed ORR per blinded, independent review according to the revised International
Neuroblastoma Response Criteria (INRC) was 34% (95% CI: 20%, 51%), with 23% of the 13 responders having an observed DOR of 6 months. The
primary efficacy analysis in Trial 12-230 included 22 patients with prior anti-GD2 treatment, and 4 (18%) of these patients demonstrated a
response (95% CI 5%, 40%). Given the limited number of patients with prior anti-GD2 exposure, the exploratory analyses of ORR in this
subpopulation should be interpreted with caution; however, the results suggest that naxitamab has some clinical activity in patients with prior
anti-GD2 antibody exposure. The difference in the point estimates of ORR between the two trials may be explained by the higher percentage of
relapsed patients, who are more heavily pre-treated, in Trial 12-230.
Assessment of DOR was limited by administration of additional anticancer therapies subsequent to achieving a complete or partial response in
patients in both trials. Neither study was designed to include scheduled assessments of response after completion of treatment with
naxitamab, and Trial 201 is currently ongoing. Additional clinical data from Trial 201 may provide additional data to inform durability of
responses.
Although the investigational naxitamab used in the two trials supporting efficacy and safety in this application used different manufacturing
processes, the Office of Biotechnology Products (OBP) determined that the products used in each trial were generally comparable, although

direct comparability of potency was not possible given differences in potency assays used for each product (refer to page 32 of the OBP

Safety: The safety of naxitamab in combination with GM-CSF was evaluated in a subpopulation of patients with relapsed or refractory
neuroblastoma high-risk neuroblastoma in bone or bone marrow, from two open-label, single arm studies, Study 201 (n=25) and Study 12-230
(n=72). In both studies, patients received naxitamab as three separate intravenous infusions of 3 mg/kg (Days 1,3 and 5) in the first week of a 4­
week cycle. Patients also received GM-CSF subcutaneously at 250 µg/m2/day on Days -4 to 0 and at 500 µg/m2/day on Days 1 to 5. No deaths
due to an adverse event were reported. Cumulative safety data indicated that serious adverse reactions occurred in 32% of patients in Trial 201
and 40% of patients in Trial 12-230. Serious adverse reactions in occurring in > 5% of patients in Trial 201 included anaphylactic reaction (12%)
and pain (8%); in Trial 12-230 serious adverse reactions occurring in >5% of patients included hypertension (14%), hypotension (11%), and
pyrexia (8%).
Naxitamab can cause serious infusion-related reactions and neurologic toxicity, including severe pain, transverse myelitis and reversible
posterior encephalopathy syndrome (RPLS). Given the potential severity of these adverse reactions and the occurrence of infusion related
reactions and pain in most patients in both trials, a boxed warning was included in Danyelza product labelling for both infusion reactions and
neurotoxicity. FDA’s pooled term analysis of infusion-related reactions included the following terms: hypotension, bronchospasm, flushing,
wheezing, stridor, urticaria, dyspnea, pyrexia, infusion related reaction, face edema, swelling face, orbital edema, periorbital edema, lip edema,
lip swelling, mouth edema, tongue edema, swollen tongue, mouth swelling, angioedema, respiratory tract edema, chills, hypoxia, pruritis, rash
macular, rash maculo-papular, rash erythematous, rash, and respiratory distress. The analysis was restricted to events which occurred the day
of the infusion of naxitamab or the day following an infusion.
Additionally, hypertension is described in the Warnings and Precautions section of product labeling. The most common adverse reactions
(≥25%) to naxitamab were infusion-related reaction, pain, tachycardia, vomiting, cough, nausea, diarrhea, decreased appetite, hypertension,
fatigue, erythema multiforme, peripheral neuropathy, urticaria, pyrexia, headache, injection site reaction, edema, anxiety, localized edema and
irritability. The most common Grade 3 or 4 laboratory abnormalities (≥5% in either study) were decreased lymphocytes, decreased neutrophils,
decreased hemoglobin, decreased platelet count, decreased potassium, increased alanine aminotransferase, decreased glucose, decreased
calcium, decreased albumin, decreased sodium and decreased phosphate.

Clinically relevant adverse reactions in ≤10% of patients who received naxitamab with GM-CSF in either study included apnea, hypopnea,
generalized edema, peripheral edema, device related infection, anxiety, unresponsiveness to stimuli, and somnolence. Events of apnea,
hypopnea, unresponsiveness and somnolence may be related to concomitant administration of opioids or to co-occurring events of severe
infusion reactions, such as severe hypotension.
The safety population included patients 1 to 24 years of age across both studies, with a median age of 5 years in both studies. Although
relatively few young adult patients and very young patients (2 years of age or younger) were enrolled, exploratory analyses of safety trends did
not demonstrate a significant difference in safety across groups.
Overall benefit:risk assessment: Given the rarity of relapsed or refractory high-risk neuroblastoma and the lack of an approved or standard
treatment, a randomized trial of naxitamab is not likely to be feasible in this population. The review team considers that the ORR in the two
trials, along with the observed duration of responses, in patients treated with naxitamab is sufficient to establish clinical benefit in the rare
group of patients with high risk neuroblastoma. Data from additional patients in this patient population including ORR and duration of
response, may be required to confirm clinical benefit. The risks associated with naxitamab appear acceptable in the setting of patients with
relapsed or refractory neuroblastoma who have no approved therapies. Naxitamab is expected to be administered in infusion centers with
intensive monitoring and the ability to quickly respond to events such as severe infusion reactions. In addition, the existence of previously
approved products in the US (dinutuximab) with a similar mechanism of action and toxicity profile means that the majority of pediatric
oncologists administering naxitamab will have familiarity with expected toxicities associated with anti-GD2 antibodies. The review team
considered the available safety and pharmacokinetic data, as well as the unmet medical need across age groups, in determining the age range
(pediatric patients 1 year and older, as well as adult patients) included in the indication statement.
In the opinion of the review team, naxitamab, in combination with GM-CSF, has a favorable benefit-risk profile for the treatment of pediatric
patients 1 year of age and older and adult patients with relapsed or refractory high-risk neuroblastoma in the bone or bone marrow who have
demonstrated a partial response, minor response, or stable disease to prior therapy.

Conclusions and Reasons

• Neuroblastoma is the most common extracranial tumor in
children, with approximately 800 cases diagnosed each year in
the United States (American Cancer Society website, 2020).
• More than 50% of these patients present with distant metastasis
(Stage 4, high-risk neuroblastoma) at the time of diagnosis,
typically in the bone and bone marrow (Matthay, 1997).
• Ten to 50% of patients with high-risk neuroblastoma do not
achieve a remission after induction/consolidation therapy and
are classified as refractory; 50% to 60% of those who complete
the treatment will relapse (Cole, 2012; Marris, 2007; Cotterill,
2000).
• The two-year post-progression/relapse survival rates range from
8% to 29%, and 10-year overall survival (OS) rates are
approximately 14% (Cotterill, 2000).

Relapsed or refractory high-risk neuroblastoma
in the bone or bone marrow is a serious and
life threatening disease with a high unmet
medical need.

• There are no FDA approved treatments for relapsed or refractory
neuroblastoma with bone or bone marrow involvement (PDQ,
2020).
• Patients are encouraged to enroll on clinical trials, are treated
with off-label treatment or with palliative care.
• Off label treatments include the following options (these data
has not been reviewed by FDA):
− Chemotherapy with immunotherapy (temozolomide/
irinotecan/dinutuximab); reported ORR 53% in a 25 patient

All currently available treatment options
are palliative and are associated with
significant toxicity.
Safe and effective treatments for this
highly morbid condition are needed.

2 Therapeutic Context
Analysis of Condition
The Applicant’s Position:
Neuroblastoma is a rare, almost exclusively pediatric cancer. Neuroblastoma is the most
common extracranial tumor in children, with about 800 cases diagnosed each year in the
United States[1] The majority of patients are diagnosed before the age of 2 years (60%), and
almost all are diagnosed before the age of 10 years (97%).[2] More than 50% of these patients
present with distant metastasis (Stage 4) at the time of diagnosis, typically in the bone and
bone marrow.[3-5]
Clinical course and patient outcome are linked to the patient’s risk categorization at the time of
diagnosis. Risk classification is by the International Neuroblastoma Risk Group (INRG)
Classification System.[6] Patients diagnosed with low-risk neuroblastoma (Stages 1, 2, and 4S,
per the International Neuroblastoma Response Criteria [INRC]) or intermediate-risk
neuroblastoma (Stage 3) have high survival rates, exceeding 95%. However, less than 50% of
patients with high-risk disease achieve long-term survival.[6]
Among patients with high-risk neuroblastoma, 10% to 50% do not achieve a remission after
induction/consolidation therapy and are classified as refractory[3, 4, 7-9] and 50% to 60% of
those who complete the treatment will relapse.[3, 4]
For patients with high-risk neuroblastoma who relapse after induction/consolidation therapy,
the sites of relapse are most commonly bone and bone marrow (BM). BM invasion is associated
with a poor prognosis.[5, 10] The time to disease progression is another important predictor of
outcome; in a follow-up analysis of relapsed patients, London et al. reported that patients at
the highest risk of death were those who relapsed between 6 and 18 months after initial
diagnosis (peak, approximately 12 months).[11]
No standard of care currently exists for the treatment of patients with refractory or relapsed
neuroblastoma, and the outcomes have been disappointingly poor for decades; the two-year
post-progression/relapse survival rates range from 8% to 29%, and 10-year overall survival (OS)
rates are around 14%.[5, 12]
30
Version date: July 24, 2019 (ALL NDA/BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant
and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 4707829

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761171}
{DANYELZA, naxitamab-gqgk}

Hence, there is a large, unmet medical need for more effective treatments for patients with
refractory or relapsed neuroblastoma with disease in bone and/or bone marrow.

The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the description provided by the Applicant.

Analysis of Current Treatment Options

The Applicant’s Position:
Standard induction therapies for high-risk neuroblastoma include surgery and five to six cycles
of induction chemotherapy with agents such as cisplatin, cyclophosphamide, doxorubicin,
etoposide, topotecan, or vincristine. Consolidation therapy includes myeloablative
chemotherapy regimens (e.g., carboplatin-etoposide-melphalan, busulfan melphalan, or
thiotepa-cyclophosphamide) with stem cell transplantation (SCT) and external-beam
radiotherapy. Postconsolidation therapy to treat minimal residual disease (MRD) includes
immunotherapy (e.g., dinutuximab) and cytokines (GM-CSF, interleukin-2), plus isotretinoin.[6,
13]
A patient who does not respond fully to induction therapy or who relapses during or after
consolidation/postconsolidation therapy can receive salvage treatment. Widely used salvage
chemotherapy regimens combine topoisomerase I inhibitors (e.g., topotecan or irinotecan) with
either alkylators (e.g., cyclophosphamide or temozolomide) or topoisomerase II inhibitors (e.g.,
doxorubicin or etoposide). If the topoisomerase I inhibitor–based regimens are ineffective,
other chemotherapy salvage regimens available include ifosfamide, carboplatin, and etoposide.
Patients with refractory or relapsed neuroblastoma have poor prognoses after incomplete
response to or relapse after frontline therapy, respectively.[3] Particularly, there is an unmet
medical need for effective treatment of refractory and relapsed patients with disease in bone
and/or bone marrow after chemotherapy, who have a high probability of developing
progressive disease with no approved treatment options.
Because there are no approved treatment options for these patients, clinical trials are ongoing
with investigational therapies for the treatment of relapsed or refractory neuroblastoma.
Investigational therapies include CAR T-cell therapy, vaccines, antibody therapies (including
anti-GD2 antibodies and checkpoint inhibitors), tyrosine kinase inhibitors, histone deacetylase
inhibitors, and ornithine decarboxylase inhibitors.
Hence, the treatment of relapsed and refractory neuroblastoma remains an area of unmet
medical need.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees that there are no approved therapeutic options for patients with high-risk
neuroblastoma that is relapsed or refractory. Treatment options commonly used at the time of
recurrence (which are not approved for this indication) may include chemotherapy as described
above, 131I-MIBG alone or in combination with other therapy, or chemotherapy in combination
with anti-GD2 therapies (PDQ Pediatric Treatment Editorial Board, 2020) .

4 Significant Issues from Other Review Disciplines Pertinent to Clinical
Conclusions on Efficacy and Safety
Office of Scientific Investigations (OSI)
One clinical site, Memorial Sloan Kettering Cancer Center (MSKCC) was selected for
inspections because all patients enrolled in Trial 12-230 and 8 of 25 patients enrolled in the
safety population of Trial 201 were treated at this site. The only other site at which more
than one patient in the efficacy population for Trial 201 was treated was Hospital Sant Joan
de Deu, located in Spain, which enrolled 16 of 25 patients in the safety population. Given
the challenges associated with international travel during the COVID-19 pandemic and the
ability to inspect the MSKCC site (Principal Investigator Dr. Brian Kushner) which generated
the majority of the clinical data across studies, inspection of the clinical site in Spain was not
deemed critical to the application review.
Review of inspections of the imaging facility used for independent central assessment of
(b) (4)
ORR and DOR,
, were last completed in July 2018 and had been
inspected three times since 2012. Each inspection had a compliance classification of No
Action Indicated. Therefore, inspection of the imaging facility was not deemed necessary for
this application.
The inspection of the MSKCC clinical site conducted by the Office of Scientific Investigations
revealed no significant findings. The studies appear to have been conducted adequately and
there was no evidence of underreporting of serious adverse events or significant protocol
deviations. Based on the inspection, the data generated by the inspected clinical
investigator appear to be acceptable in support of the BLA.

5 Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
Executive Summary
The FDA’s Assessment:
Naxitamab (hu3F8-IgG1) is a humanized monoclonal antibody against the ganglioside GD2,
which is a member of the b-series gangliosides and an adhesion molecule widely expressed on
the cell membrane in neuroectoderm-derived tumors; the established pharmacological class is
GD2-binding monoclonal antibody.. An earlier version of the antibody studied clinically, a
mouse anti-GD2 monoclonal antibody coded m3F8, had promising clinical outcomes in patients
with neuroblastoma; however, m3F8 also had significant immunogenicity in patients, leading
the original investigators to develop the humanized version of m3F8, naxitamab. Naxitamab has
the same epitope-targeting region of the murine antibody. In binding studies, naxitamab bound
to human GD2 with a KD of 19.8 nM. Naxitamab had no off-target binding to other gangliosides
including GM2, GD1a, GT1b, GD3, and GQ1b, and had minimal binding to ganglioside GD1b
(4.5% compared to maximal binding to GD2).
In the absence of human serum or effector cells, naxitamab had direct cytotoxicity against the
GD2 expressing human neuroblastoma cell line LAN-1 with EC50 values of 5 µg/mL
(approximately 29 times lower than the Cmax of 147 µg/mL in patients treated with naxitamab
for multiple cycles on Days 1, 3, and 5 at 9.0 mg/kg). In antibody dependent cellular cytotoxicity
(ADCC) assays, including assays using human peripheral blood mononuclear cells (PBMCs),
polymorphonuclear leukocytes (PMNs), or CD16 or CD32 expressing NK cell lines as effector
cells, naxitamab showed significantly increased activity against high GD2-expressing LAN-1
neuroblastoma target cells and 5 other GD2-expressing cell lines compared to the original
murine version of the antibody. Conversely, naxitamab had mild decreases in CDC-mediated
cytotoxicity compared to the murine parent antibody. No tested anti-GD2 antibody mediated
significant ADCC or CDC activity against a low GD2-expressing cell line. Investigators also
showed that the addition of granulocyte-macrophage colony stimulating factor (GM-CSF) to
PBMC cultures resulted in concentration-dependent increases in m3F8-mediated ADCC against
human neuroblastoma cell lines of up to 30% compared to m3F8 alone, providing support for
the use of GM-CSF in the clinical dosing regimen for naxitamab.
In an in vivo study, twice-weekly IV naxitamab administration resulted in dose-dependent antitumor activity in mice bearing LAN-1 subcutaneous tumors and decreased tumor size to a
greater extent than m3F8 given on the same schedule. Further, Bergman and colleagues (1993)
showed that m3F8 alleviated paraparesis caused by epidurally-implanted metastatic
39
Version date: July 24, 2019 (ALL NDA/BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant
and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 4707829

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761171}
{DANYELZA, naxitamab-gqgk}

neuroblastoma in a rat model due to m3F8-induced shrinkage of tumors in the epidural space.
The Applicant submitted additional in vivo data from publications using a surrogate anti-GD2
antibody targeting a separate GD2 epitope to further explore the known naxitamab-related
clinical side effect of pain. Intravenous or intrathecal administration of a murine IgG2a isotype
anti-GD2 monoclonal antibody (14.G2a) to adult immunocompetent Sprague Dawley rats led to
mechanical allodynia, confirming the enhanced pain effect of anti-GD2 administration in
animals.
The initial clinical trials investigating the activity and dosing of naxitamab were conducted on
the basis of previous clinical data with the murine parent antibody rather than supported by
toxicology data with the clinical candidate. As clinical development proceeded, FDA advised the
manufacturer that the Agency would expect additional animal toxicology data to support a
future BLA submission. The investigators asserted that nude rats rather than a non-human
primate would be a reasonable toxicology model and confirmed the presence of NK cells and
PMN in these animals prior to conducting toxicology studies. Investigators reasoned that the
use of nude rats rather than fully immune competent animals would allow for avoidance of the
formation of anti-drug antibodies that would prevent long-term assessment of a human
antibody in a rodent model. To help address FDA concerns regarding the use of
immunodeficient rats as pharmacologically relevant species for toxicological testing of
naxitamab, Y-mAbs also evaluated the ability of naxitamab to mediate ADCC using effector cells
from nude rats against rat GD2-expressing target cells and compared these effects to
naxitamab-induced ADCC using human effector cells against human GD2-expressing target cells.
Naxitamab did bind to both rat GD2 and to splenocytes from nude rats; however, in the
presence of nude rat splenocyte effector cells, naxitamab did not induce ADCC against GD2­
expressing rat PC12 cells and induced only ~10% cytotoxicity in GD2-expressing rat C6 cells.
Naxitamab induced approximately 22% ADCC in human LAN-1/rat splenocyte co-cultures
whereas there was 55% ADCC against the same cell line in the presence of human NK cells,
indicating that the rat/rat and rat/human systems underpredicted the activity of the
human/human system. Naxitamab also led to 24 to 54% ADCC at 30 and 100 µg/mL,
respectively, against another GD2-expressing human neuroblastoma cell line, IMR-32.
Additional studies to support selection of the nude rat for use in pivotal toxicity studies
included evaluation of the reactivity of naxitamab in tissue cross-reactivity studies using
peripheral nerve, skin, spinal cord, and brain sections from human, Sprague-Dawley rat, and
nude rats. Staining with naxitamab occurred in the cerebral cortex, cerebellum, spinal cord, and
peripheral nerve in tissues, and skin from human and both rat strains. Significantly stronger
positive staining occurred in human tissues compared to both rat strains.

40
Version date: July 24, 2019 (ALL NDA/BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant
and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 4707829

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761171}
{DANYELZA, naxitamab-gqgk}

Y-mAbs conducted a GLP-compliant 13-week toxicology study in nude rats. Rats received
intravenous naxitamab for 4 cycles of 3 treatment days per cycle (every other day on Days 1, 3,
and 5) at doses of 0, 10, or 50 mg/kg. Exposures achieved in rats at the 50 mg/kg dose level
were 32 (males) and 25 (females) times higher than the AUC in patients (2386 µg*h/mL) at the
once every 3 days per cycle clinical dose of 9 mg/kg. Multiple deaths occurred in all dosing
groups including the control group, with mortality rates of 13% in controls, 20% in the 10 mg/kg
group, and 13% in the 50 mg/kg group; the causes of death were undetermined and without
notable patterns of clinical signs or pathology, suggesting that they were not related to the testarticle. In surviving animals, naxitamab-related toxicity was generally mild. Naxitamab caused
decreased body weight gain of up to 12% in both the 10 and 50 mg/kg groups compared to
control animals which persisted into the recovery phase. Rats treated with both doses of
naxitamab showed minimal increases in reticulocytes, neutrophils, lymphocytes, eosinophils,
basophils, platelets, and APTT with no dependence on dose level, and decreased prothrombin
in the 50 mg/kg group as well as minimal increases in urea and potassium. All clinical chemistry
and hematology findings recovered by the end of the recovery period. Minor decreases in
organ weights of the thyroid/parathyroid occurred at both dose levels. Gross pathology findings
were limited to the stomach with depression and thickening noted at both dose levels which
was further supported by histopathology findings of hyperplasia, inflammation, and erosion of
the stomach. Additional histopathology findings included minimal to moderate inflammatory
cell infiltrate/inflammation of the plantar and tibial nerves in treated males. Evaluation of
nociception (pain response) using an analgesiometer and application of increasing pressure on
the paw between a flat surface and a blunt pointer showed a mild increase in pain threshold,
which is in contrast to the expected pharmacological response due to treatment with an anti­
GD2 antibody, and in contrast to the responses shown by Sprague Dawley rats treated with a
surrogate anti-GD2 antibody as reviewed in the pharmacology section as well as clinical findings
of increased pain sensitivity. The lack of increased pain response in adult nude rats and a similar
lack of a clear effect in a non-GLP dose range finding study in juvenile nude rats coupled with
the in vitro ADCC data using nude rat effector and target cells showing significantly reduced
activity compared to the human counterparts suggests that the immunodeficient nude rat does
not fully recapitulate the activity of drug and therefore does not appear to be a reasonable
strain for assessing the toxicological effects of naxitamab.
Y-mAbs did not conduct dedicated studies to assess fertility or carcinogenicity and these studies
are not warranted to support the development of a drug intended to treat patients with
advanced cancer. In addition, because the age of the proposed patient population is primarily
≤5 years old, FDA did not request an embryo-fetal development study to support the use of
naxitamab. As IgG1 antibodies can cross the placenta with increasing frequency as pregnancy
persists and because naxitamab can bind to normal GD2 expressing cells of neuroectodermal
41

and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761171}
{DANYELZA, naxitamab-gqgk}

origin, including the central nervous system and peripheral nerves, a warning for embryo-fetal
toxicity is included in the label.
Given the concerns regarding the model used for the toxicological assessment (nude rats), the
nonclinical team cannot conclude that the toxicity of naxitamab has been adequately tested in
animal models to look for any consequences related chronic administration of this product.
Given the unusual development history, the application appears approvable based on the
strength of the available clinical data in the proposed patient population with few clinical
options; however, the nonclinical team will request a post-marketing requirement (PMR) to
further explore the toxicity of naxitamab given for a minimum of 2 cycles in a relevant animal
species. Because of the age of the intended patient population and the lack of significant
clinical experience in adult patients and the focus on potential effects on nerve development,
this PMR study should be conducted in juvenile animals.

A. In Vitro Studies
Binding
In Study #YMAB_GD2_AFFI_BIAC_ARP_02, naxitamab (hu3F8-IgG1) bound to human GD2 with
greater affinity than either of the 14.18-based anti-GD2 antibodies, dinutuximab beta or
Unituxin (KDs of 19.8 nM vs. 251 and 169 nM, respectively) as measured via surface plasmon
resonance (SPR). In addition, the Applicant cited data from Cheung et al. 2012, showing that
compared to alternate anti-GD2 antibodies 14.G2a and ME36.1, both humanized (hu3F8-IgG1
[naxitamab] and hu3F8-IgG4) and chimeric 3F8 antibodies had higher affinity for human GD2 as

Antibody-mediated toxicity
Cheung et al. (2012) showed that in the absence of human serum or human effector cells
naxitamab had cytotoxic activity against the GD2-expressing human neuroblastoma cell line,
LAN-1, with an EC50 of 5 µg/mL, comparable or more potent compared to the previous clinical
candidate m3F8 and a murine IgG2a isotype variant of another clinically studies anti-GD2
targeted antibody, 14.G2a (2 and 47 µg/mL, respectively). In addition, using a chromium release
assay investigators compared naxitamab-induced antibody-dependent cellular cytotoxicity
(ADCC) relative to parent antibody m3F8-induced ADCC using human peripheral blood
mononuclear cells (PBMCs) in the presence of IL-2 or human polymorphonuclear leukocytes
(PMN) as effector cells and LAN-1 cells as target cells at a 50:1 ratio in the presence of GM-CSF.
Similar assays were conducted using IL-2 independent natural killer (NK-92MI) cells expressing
human CD16 or CD32 Fc receptors at a ratio of 20:1 and without the addition of cytokines. In
addition, the authors assessed GD2-directed antibody-mediated complement dependent
cytotoxicity (CDC) compared to the murine parent antibody for naxitamab (m3F8). Relative to
m3F8, naxitamab (hu3F8-IgG1) showed significant increases ADCC activity against the LAN-1
neuroblastoma cell in line in the PBMC and PMN assays as well as all other assays assessing
ADCC (Table 3). Conversely, naxitamab had decreased CDC activity compared to the murine
parent. The IgG4 isotype of hu3F8 did not have appreciable activity in any assay conducted
(data not shown).

Similarly, Munn and Cheung, 1990 showed that the m3F8 antibody was able to mediate
antibody dependent cellular phagocytosis (ADCP). Investigators cultured human monocytes
with CSF to enhance macrophage maturation and labeled cells with anti-CD14/CD-11b-PE
antibodies then incubated these monocytes/macrophages with PKH2 (green fluorescent dye)­
labelled GD2-expressing neuroblastoma cells (LAN-1) for 8 to 24 hours. The authors then
measured internalization of green dye-labeled cells by microscopy or flow cytometry. In the
presence of m3F8, there were high levels of target cell engulfment (data not shown). The
authors did not include the clinical candidate, naxitamab, in this assessment.
Species Selection
To help justify use of the immune deficient nude rat as a relevant model for the toxicological
assessment of naxitamab, in Study # NM52RT the Applicant evaluated the ability of naxitamab
to mediate ADCC using effector cells from nude rats against rat GD2-expressing target cells and
compared the results to naxitamab-induced ADCC using human effector cells against human
GD2-expressing target cells.
Evaluation of GD2 levels in GD2-expressing rat cell lines (B35 neuronal neuroblast, C6 glial
tumor, F98 glioblastoma, PC12 adrenal pheochromocytoma) via flow cytometry led
investigators to choose C6 and PC12 cells as rat target cells. Based on mean fluorescent
intensity, naxitamab showed higher binding to C6 and PC12 cells compared to B35 and F98 rat
cells but binding intensity was significantly lower on rat cells compared to human
neuroblastoma LAN-1 cells known to express GD2 (Table 7). In addition, investigators assessed
binding of naxitamab to natural killer (NK) cells from nude rats (identified using an anti-rat NK
cell receptor CD161 antibody) in nude rat splenocytes. Double binding of naxitamab and CD161

While the ADCC data does suggest that there was some ADCC activity by rat splenocytes, the
activity using nude rat cells against rat targets was less robust than ADCC against human cells in
the presence of human NK cells (maximum 10% for nude rat SC against rat GD-2 expressing
cells vs. maximum 55% for human PBMCs against high expressing human GD-2 LAN-1 cells).
This data did not provide clear support for the use of the nude rat as a species for toxicological
assessment of naxitamab.
B. In Vivo Studies
Athymic mice implanted subcutaneously with LAN-1 tumors began twice weekly treatment with
naxitamab at dose levels of 20 or 100 µg for a total of eight doses after tumors reached a
volume of ~200 mm3. Treatment with naxitamab had minimal activity at the 20 µg dose level,
with increased activity at 100 µg; m3F8 at 200µg on the same schedule had no clear effect
(Cheung et al. 2012).

Figure 4: Anti-tumor activity of naxitamab and m3F8 in adult mice bearing subcutaneous LAN­

(Excerpted from Cheung et al. 2012)

Bergman and colleagues (1993) analyzed the activity of m3F8 in an epidural metastatic
neuroblastoma model in nude rats using neurological outcome as measured by limb weakness
as an endpoint. Paraparesis developed in 79% of rats 19 days post thoracic epidural
implantation of human neuroblastoma cells (NMB7). Histological analysis of a single rat on Day
19 showed that the neuroblastoma tumors occupied approximately 50% of the epidural space;
the remaining rats began treatment with m3F8 or 131I-m3F8 at the initial sign of weakness with
a 2nd dose 5-7 days later before necropsy 10 days post last treatment. Rats receiving no
antibody or control antibodies (Table 10) became paraplegic and remained so until death,
except for 2 (10%) rats with improvement from Grade 5 limb weakness to Grade 2/3 and no
pathological evidence of tumors at necropsy despite evidence of myelopathy. Rats
administered intravenous m3F8 (5 mg/kg) later showed improved neurological function (82% vs
10% controls), with initial improvement starting at 3.8 days and maximum improvement by 7.1
days. Six rats receiving m3F8 rats recovered normal neurological function which persisted until
necropsy. In rats treated with radiolabeled 131I-m3F8 (5 mCi/kg 131I ~3.125 mCi/mg m3F8), 100%
of the treated rats showed improved neurological function from Grade 3.6 to 2.5 by Day 6.8,
but 7 out of 9 rats (78%) deteriorated again prior to necropsy. Tumors contained a mean of
1.69% injected dose of 131I-m3F8/g tumor weight (tumor weights ranged from 5 to 21 mg), and
tumor to blood ratios varied from 0.7 to 16.2 in individual animals (mean = 4.46).

The Applicant submitted a series of publications using a surrogate anti-GD2 antibody targeting
a separate GD2 epitope from naxitamab to explore naxitamab’s known clinical side of effect of
pain. In Sorkin et al. 2002, (Sorkin LS, 2002) intravenous or intrathecal administration of an anti­
GD2 monoclonal antibody (14G2a) to adult Sprague Dawley rats led to mechanical allodynia.
Rats treated intravenously with 1 mg/kg of the antibody showed significant decrease in
mechanical threshold for paw withdrawal in the 3 hours post administration (Figure 5). This
allodynia was, however, limited to mechanical stimulation, as there was no effect on thermal
withdrawal threshold following 1 or 3 mg/kg of intravenous anti-GD2 or 0.05 ng of intrathecal
anti-GD2 (Figure 6). Control murine IgG2a had no effect on mechanical allodynia.

Secondary Pharmacology
The Applicant’s Position:
Y-mAbs has not conducted any studies dedicated to investigating secondary pharmacodynamics
with naxitamab.
The FDA’s Assessment:
Relevant pharmacology studies are discussed in the primary pharmacology section. Studies
extensively investigating activity unrelated to the antibody target are not commonly expected
for monoclonal antibodies.

Safety Pharmacology
The Applicant’s Position:
In accordance with ICH S9 guidance, Nonclinical Evaluation for Anticancer Pharmaceuticals
(October 2009), Y-mAbs has not conducted any standalone safety pharmacology studies with
naxitamab.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees that stand-alone safety pharmacology studies are not expected for an antibody
intended for the treatment of patients with advanced cancer. In general cardiovascular, CNS,
and respiratory safety endpoints are included in general toxicity studies. See the general
toxicity assessment for further details.

The Applicant’s Position:
The nonclinical pharmacokinetic studies were designed to characterize the absorption and
clearance of naxitamab after either single or repeated dosing by intravenous injection in mice
and rats and to characterize distribution of naxitamab at target and off-target sites by
evaluating immunoreactivity and biodistribution of naxitamab using in vitro and in vivo test
methods.
Single-dose pharmacokinetic studies evaluating the absorption and blood clearance of
naxitamab were conducted in mice. Total systemic clearance was measured after a single
intravenous injection of 131I naxitamab in C57BL/6 and BALB/c athymic nude mice. Systemic
clearance of 131I naxitamab was substantially faster in nude mice when compared with that in
C57BL/6 mice. In a second study, serum pharmacokinetics of naxitamab were investigated after
an intravenous injection in nude mice. In this study, the serum terminal half-life of naxitamab
was calculated to be 17 hours.
In repeat-dose toxicity studies in nude rats, serum pharmacokinetics were measured after the
final intravenous dose of naxitamab in each of Cycles 1 and 2 of a 6-week study or Cycles 1 and
4 of a 13-week study in athymic Hsd:RH-Foxn1rnu nude rats. In both studies, the extent of
exposure after naxitamab administration was characterized by nonlinear, dose-dependent
kinetics over the dose range tested; additionally, after either two or four cycles of treatment
with naxitamab, there was evidence of accumulation in the latter dosing cycle. In general,
exposure in females was slightly lower than in males.

Version date: July 24, 2019 (ALL NDA/BLA reviews)

and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 4707829

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761171}
{DANYELZA, naxitamab-gqgk}

The potential distribution and affinity of naxitamab to organs of interest were evaluated by in
vitro and in vivo test methods. In an in vitro tissue immunoreactivity study, the relative affinity
of naxitamab was assessed in selected frozen human tissue sections (normal organ and muscle
tissue, and neuroblastoma tissues) to better characterize the potential distribution and
reactivity of the antibody across tissues of interest. In general, aside from tumor tissues,
naxitamab showed relatively strong affinity to the brain, cerebellum, and spinal cord from
healthy tissue sections and weak affinity to the lung, sigmoid colon, stomach, and testes. In an
in vivo distribution study, a single intravenous dose of 131I naxitamab was administered to
athymic nude mice harboring xenografted subcutaneous human neuroblastoma tumors. The
mouse model used in the biodistribution study, athymic nude mice harboring subcutaneous
human neuroblastoma xenografts, was the same model used for in vivo primary
pharmacodynamics studies evaluating the efficacy of naxitamab in an animal model of disease.
In distribution studies in mice, naxitamab preferentially accumulated in tumor tissue compared
with nontumor tissues.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s analysis of distribution of intravenous 131I-hu3F8-IgG1
(naxitamab) and 131I-m3F8 (100 µCi) in adult female athymic nude mice bearing subcutaneous
LAN-1 neuroblastoma tumors showing accumulation mostly in tumors (29% of injected
dose/gram of mouse), followed by blood, spleen, liver, adrenal, bladder, lung, and skin (2.1­
4.2%), though use of a high GD-2 expressing implanted human tumor may exaggerate the
distribution of the antibody to tumor vs normal tissue.
FDA could not confirm the difference in absorption and clearance of naxitamab in the C5BL6 vs.
nude mouse; however, this data was not critical for the nonclinical safety assessment of this
product. Studies investigating metabolism and excretion are not commonly expected for
monoclonal antibodies.
FDA agrees that tissue cross reactivity studies showed binding to peripheral nerve, cerebellum,
spinal cord, frontal lobe, and pons. Basal epithelium of the skin was another important target
in some studies. In additional studies to those discussed above, the Applicant determined the
tissue cross-reactivity of naxitamab (hu3F8) in Study #PK86DG and compared the crossreactivity of naxitamab (hu3F8) and Unituxin (an alternate anti-GD2 antibody targeting a
different epitope) in Study #DL25SP using frozen tissues from humans (3 donors), SpragueDawley (SD) rats, and Hsd:RH Foxn1rnu (nude) rats. Tissues were limited to skin containing
peripheral nerve, tibial peripheral nerve, cerebellum, spinal cord, frontal lobe, and pons from
each species. Positive staining occurred in cerebral cortex, cerebellum, spinal cord, and

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761171}

peripheral nerve tissues for naxitamab from each species, representing on target binding.
There were no significant differences in intensity or distribution of the staining between the
two antibodies in human tissues. Naxitamab staining in human tissues had increased intensity
compared to tissues from either rat strain in both studies. Additional naxitamab staining
included basal epithelium of the skin in human tissues and follicular epithelium of the skin in
both rat species in both studies. Non-specific staining with naxitamab and Unituxin occurred at
levels similar to those that observed with isotype controls in the white matter from neural
tissues analyzed from human donors. No non-specific staining occurred in either rat species
using naxitamab.

Toxicology
General Toxicology
Data (presented by Applicant):
Study title/ Study number:
A 13-Week Toxicity Study by Intravenous Infusion Administration to Hsd:RH-Foxn1rnu
Nude Rats Followed by a 4-Week Recovery Period (YN70HM)
Key Study Findings
•

•
•

The intravenous infusion administration of naxitamab (an anti-GD2 mAb) to
Hsd:RH-Foxn1rnu nude rats, over four cycles of 3 treatment days, up to 50 mg/kg
per occasion was generally well tolerated over the 13-week dosing period. Body
weight gain was slightly affected with treatment; however, no other treatmentrelated effects were noted with respect to clinical evaluations (including general
health and injection-site examinations), food consumption, nociception
response, or ophthalmoscopic examination.
Treatment-related findings on organ weights and on hematology and blood
chemistry parameters were considered nonadverse. In general, these effects
were mild, lacking dose-related trends, and reversible.
Adverse findings in histopathology evaluations of the stomach, including
hyperplasia with inflammation in the glandular mucosa of the stomach in males
at 50 mg/kg per occasion and marked erosions of the glandular mucosa of the
stomach in females at greater than or equal to 10 mg/kg per occasion, resulted
in the NOAEL to be established at 10 mg/kg/day (30 mg/kg per cycle) in male rats
only, corresponding to a Cmax of 0.282 mg/mL and AUC0-216 h of 19.9 mg h/mL

Observations and Results: changes from control
Parameters

Major Findings

Mortality

No treatment-related mortality occurred. There were 7 unscheduled
deaths during the study, including 2 control animals, 3 animals that
were treated at 10 mg/kg/day, and 2 animals that were treated at 50
mg/kg/day. Of the deaths, 5 occurred during the treatment phase of
the study, and 2 occurred during the recovery phase. All were
considered incidental and not test article–related.

Clinical Signs

There were no other clinical signs of note, including any from general
health examinations or injection site observations, that were
considered to be outside of normal limits.

Body Weights

Compared with controls, there was an apparent trend of slower rates
of weight gain in naxitamab-treated males at both dose levels (88%­
94% of control animal’s weight) and in females treated at both dose
levels (89%-91% of control animal’s weight) from Days 1 to 95 of the
study. In the recovery phase, mean weight value gains were also
reduced in males treated at either dose level. On the basis of these
trends, body weight was considered to be affected by naxitamab
treatment.

Ophthalmoscopy

There were no treatment-related ophthalmoscopic findings.

Hematology

Nonadverse, reversible effects on hematology parameters were
observed with treatment, including increased reticulocyte counts in
females at Week 6 (1.11×) and increased total leukocyte counts in
males at both dose levels at Week 6 (1.11-1.32×) and at Week 13
(1.33-1.43×). Dose-related increases in neutrophils, lymphocytes,
eosinophils, and basophils were observed in males at Week 6 (1.10­
1.73×) and, except for basophils, also at Week 13 (1.16-1.61×). At
Week 13, monocytes were also elevated in naxitamab-treated males
(1.70-1.87×). Shorter prothrombin times were noted in both sexes and
with either dose level at Week 6 (0.96-0.82×) and Week 13 (0.91­
0.80×). The hematological effects recovered by the end of the 4-week
recovery period after the last cycle of dosing.

Clinical Chemistry

Nonadverse effects on blood chemistry parameters were observed
after either two or four dosing cycles. At Week 13, the changes
included increased urea concentrations in both sexes at the high dose
level (1.12-1.15×) and increased glucose concentrations in males at
either dose level (1.16-1.23×). Except for glucose levels in males
treated at the high dose of naxitamab, all changes in blood chemistry
recovered by the end of the recovery period. The treatment-related

effects were generally mild, not dose related, and not associated with

FDA Additional Comment: There was a 13% increase in Potassium in
HD males at the 6 week timepoint.
Urinalysis

The composition of urine was unaffected by treatment.

Gross Pathology

The macroscopic examination performed in animals after 4 weeks of
recovery revealed changes in the stomach. Internal depressions were
observed in one female previously treated with 50 mg/kg per
occasion, two females previously treated with 10 mg/kg per occasion,
and one control female. Thickening of the stomach wall was also
observed in one female, which was previously treated with 10 mg/kg
per occasion.

Organ Weights

Nonadverse, reversible changes in organ weights were observed,
consisting of higher mean adjusted liver weights in males at the high
dose level (1.10×), lower mean adjusted thyroid and parathyroid
weights at either dose level in both sexes (0.67-0.74× in males, 0.88×
in females), and higher spleen weights in females treated at the high
dose level (1.16×). Minor changes in reproductive organ weights were
observed in both sexes at both dose levels, including increased
prostate weights (1.08-1.11×) and decreased seminal vesicle weights
(0.89-0.97×) in males and elevated uterus and cervix weights (1.08­
1.11×) in females. In general, these findings were not statistically
significant and lacked a dose-related trend. Except for the findings in
reproductive organs in animals treated at the high dose level, all
changes in organ weights recovered 4 weeks after dosing. The
persistent, minor changes in reproductive organ weights were of
unknown etiology, but they were of small magnitude and were not
associated with histological findings, suggesting that the changes were
nonadverse.

Histopathology

Treatment-related histopathology changes were confined to
inflammatory and/or hyperplastic changes of the glandular mucosa of
stomachs in males treated with 50 mg/kg per occasion, and erosions
of the glandular mucosa of the stomach in females treated with 10
mg/kg per occasion and in both sexes treated with 50 mg/kg per
occasion. In the stomach, erosion of the glandular mucosa mostly
correlated with macroscopic depressions of the glandular stomach,
and hyperplasia of the glandular mucosa mostly correlated with
macroscopic thickening of the mucosa. Erosions of the glandular
mucosa were sometimes accompanied by inflammation and/or
edema, mostly in the males. After the 4-week off-dose period, there
was a full recovery of both the macroscopic and microscopic findings

The Applicant’s Position:
The Hsd:RH-Foxn1rnu nude rat species has been used for the pivotal toxicity studies and
toxicokinetic evaluations to mitigate the potential risk of a strong antidrug antibody response,
which would limit the value of longer-term chronic toxicity studies in outbred laboratory rat
strains (e.g., Sprague-Dawley or Wistar rats). Y-mAbs evaluated on-target tissue reactivity in
two GLP-compliant studies by using selected tissues of human and rat origin to support the
species selected for toxicology studies (Studies PK86GD and DL25SP). A non-GLP in vitro study
comparing the ability of naxitamab to mediate an immune response by cells from nude rats
against rat GD2-expressing cells versus human cells against human GD2-expressing cells
showed that naxitamab induced ADCC in human and nude rat systems. The binding profile
comparisons and the functional assays conducted with cells from the nude rat species, in
addition to other factors that substantiate the use of the nude rat species in toxicology studies
(e.g., its historical use in regulatory toxicology studies and its retention of intact relevant
downstream systems, such as natural killer cells, macrophages, and the complement system),
justify the selection of the Hsd: RH-Foxn1rnu nude rat species for pivotal toxicity studies and
toxicokinetic evaluations.
The safety profile of naxitamab is based on a single-dose toxicity study in mice and repeat-dose
toxicology studies (6- and 13-week) in Hsd: RH-Foxn1rnu nude rats. In brief, for the repeat-dose
studies, in the 6-week study, nonadverse changes in hematological and biochemical parameters
and organ weights were observed and considered to be treatment-related; however, these
changes were generally minor and were not correlated with macroscopic or microscopic
findings. Because all findings were considered incidental or nonadverse in this study, the NOAEL
was determined to be 50 mg/kg/day (150 mg/kg per cycle) for both male and female rats,
corresponding to a mean (sexes combined) Cmax of 1.57 mg/mL and AUC0-216 h of 86.15 mg⋅h/mL
after two dosing cycles. In the 13-week study, adverse findings in histopathology evaluations of
the stomach resulted in the NOAEL to be established at 10 mg/kg/day (30 mg/kg per cycle) in
male rats only, corresponding to a Cmax of 0.282 mg/mL and AUC0-216 h of 19.9 mg⋅h/mL after
four dosing cycles.
In mice and rats, it was demonstrated that naxitamab had acceptable safety margin estimates
based on body weight scaling of the NOAEL to the human equivalent dose. Overall, Y-mAbs
asserts that the safety profile of naxitamab established in this focused nonclinical program,
particularly in the context of the extensive clinical history of use of naxitamab, murine 3F8, and
other anti-GD2 antibodies that demonstrates an adequate safety profile, supports the use of
naxitamab for the treatment of neuroblastoma.

Reference ID: 4707829

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761171}
{DANYELZA, naxitamab-gqgk}

The FDA’s Assessment:
While FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s description of the findings in the nude rat
toxicology study, the Agency does not agree that the nude rat adequately recapitulates the
critical toxicological attributes of the product to fully inform on the safety of naxitamab.
Naxitamab-mediated toxicities in the nude rat in the 13-week study were generally minor with
mild GI toxicity (decreased weight gain, minimal to moderate histopathological erosion in the
glandular stomach), decreased thyroid weight, and an increased incidence of inflammation of
nerves (male rats only). Similar clinical signs and pathology occurred in a 6-week intravenous
GLP-compliant study in nude rats conducted by the Sponsor in which naxitamab was
administered at 0, 10, or 50 mg/kg for 2 cycles (Week 1; Days 1, 3, 5 and Week 5; Days 29, 31,
33) including decreased weight gain, hematology and clinical chemistry changes, and
histopathology. Evaluation of nociception via foot pinch indicated no change in pain threshold
following mechanical stimulation with a blunt pointer at any dose level, as observed in the 13­
week study.
While nude mice and rats are common strains employed for pharmacological activity
assessment of drugs, particularly in tumor implantation models, they are not typically used as
the primary model for the toxicological safety assessment of either drugs or biologics. The
Applicant explained that the NK cells, neutrophils, and macrophages that drive the killing of
naxitamab-bound cells are present in nude rats and that, therefore, the use of this strain would
permit for a reasonable safety assessment in rodents even in chronic toxicity studies as the
animals would not develop anti-product antibodies that would limit exposure to the testarticle. FDA acknowledged both that these cells were present and the Applicant’s goal of
circumventing the development of anti-drug antibodies, but expressed some reservations
regarding the use of an immune deficient model to model the safety of a product with a
mechanism of action based on immune-mediated cell killing and suggested that the Applicant
conduct an in vitro study comparing the ADCC activity of naxitamab in a nude rat system vs. a
human system at several points during the clinical development of the product to help support
the use of this model. The Applicant has provided this data with the BLA submission; however,
the data shows minimal activity using nude rat cells vs. human cells as effector cells. This data,
in addition to the lack of effects on nociception demonstrated in the nude rat, suggests that this
strain does not adequately recapitulate the toxicity of naxitamab. Literature cited by the
Applicant using other antibodies targeting GD2 in fully immunocompetent rat or mouse models
demonstrates the ability of these rodents to model the allodynia observed clinically, further
suggesting that though some immune-competent rodent models may adequately predict the
toxicity of the antibody the nude rat is does not, making it an inadequate toxicology model for
this product.

Treatment with naxitamab at dose levels up to and including 50 mg/kg/occasion was
well tolerated. No premature deaths occurred during the course of the study, and there
were no changes in general clinical condition or postdosing signs that were related to

administration and no histopathological changes were apparent in the stomach of
animals given 50 mg/kg per occasion.

dose duration) would be suitable for use as the high dose level in a main juvenile toxicity

Observations and Results: changes from control
Parameters

Major Findings

Mortality

No premature deaths occurred during the course of the study.

Clinical Signs

There were no changes in general clinical condition or postdosing signs
that were related to naxitamab administration.

Body Weights

Group mean body weight gain for females given naxitamab at
50 mg/kg per occasion during the two dosing cycles was slightly lower
than control animals, with statistical significance attained for the
differences during both dosing cycles. The body weight gain of males
given 50 mg/kg per occasion was slightly lower than control animals
during the intraperitoneal dosing cycle, but statistical significance was
not attained. Overall mean body weight gain in both sexes in this dose
group during PND 12-54 was unaffected.
The body weight performance of males and females given 5/7.5/10 or
30 mg/kg per occasion was unaffected.

Gross Pathology

At scheduled termination on PND 54, one male in the 30-mg/kg per
occasion group and one female in the 5-/7.5-/10-mg/kg per occasion
group had diffuse dark areas in the glandular mucosa of the stomach.
In addition, one male in the 50-mg/kg per occasion group and one
female in the 5-/7.5-/10-mg/kg per occasion group had a liver mass.

Histopathology

Microscopic examination of the stomach of animals in the control and
50-mg/kg per occasion groups revealed no test item–related findings.
The incidence and distribution of all findings were considered to be
unrelated to treatment.

Other Evaluations

The pain threshold of males was not clearly affected by naxitamab,
although it was noted during the predose assessment that the pain
threshold of males in the 50-mg/kg per occasion group was reached at
a slightly lower force (50 g) than in controls (59 g).
In females, while there was no effect on pain threshold during the
predose assessment, following dose administration there was a clear
and dose-dependent decrease in pain threshold in the 30- or 50-mg/kg
per occasion groups.

The Applicant’s Position:
This preliminary study assessed the influence of naxitamab, when administered to juvenile
Hsd:RH-Foxn1rnu nude rats at doses up to 50 mg/kg per occasion from PND 12 over two cycles
of 3 treatment days: on Days 1, 3, and 5 (PND 12, 14, and 16) by intraperitoneal injection, and
on Days 29, 31, and 33 (PND 40, 42, and 44) by intravenous infusion for up to 60 minutes.
Based on the results obtained in this study, it was concluded that the dose level of 50 mg/kg
per occasion (limited by the maximum volume/infusion rate in the required dose duration)
would be suitable for use as the high dose level in a main juvenile toxicity study, if deemed
applicable.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA did not review this study in detail because it was a dose range finding study using a limited
number of animals in a strain that does not appear to be a model that adequately recapitulates
the toxicity of naxitamab and did not include the primary histopathology data. No deaths or
clinical observations occurred in this study, with the exception of decreased body weight gain in
the high dose group. No changes in pain thresholds were present in males and effects in
females were too highly variable to make any conclusions.

The Applicant’s Position:
Y-mAbs has not conducted genetic toxicology studies with naxitamab. In accordance with ICH
S6 and ICH S9, these studies are not considered necessary to support a marketing application
for a biotechnology-derived pharmaceutical intended to treat advanced cancer.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees.

Carcinogenicity
The Applicant’s Position:

biotechnology-derived pharmaceutical intended to treat advanced cancer.

FDA agrees.

Reproductive and Developmental Toxicology
The Applicant’s Position:
Considering the proposed patient population, it is unlikely that reproductive and
developmental studies in animals will contribute meaningful safety data relevant to the
indicated population; therefore, Y-mAbs has not conducted reproductive toxicology studies
with naxitamab.
The FDA’s Assessment:
No reproductive and developmental toxicology studies were conducted to support the
submission of the BLA for naxitamab. Based on the age of the proposed patient population, FDA
agreed that traditional reproductive toxicology studies were not warranted to support the use
of this product in the treatment of patients with maintenance phase of high-risk
neuroblastoma, as based on a review by Brodeur and Maris 2003, neuroblastoma is defined as a
pediatric cancer, with the average age of diagnosis being 18 months with about 40% of patients
diagnosed by 1 year of age, 75% by 4 years and 98% by 10 years of age.

6 Clinical Pharmacology
Executive Summary
The FDA’s Assessment:
The proposed dosing regimen for naxitamab is 3.0 mg/kg/day on Days 1, 3, and 5 of each 28­
day cycle, for a total of 9 mg/kg/cycle, in combination with GM-CSF 250 µg/m2/day on Days -4
to 0 and 500 µg/m2/day on Days 1 to 5 of each 28-day cycle. The primary efficacy endpoint
(ORR) of this dosing regimen was evaluated in Study 12-230, Study 201, and Study 2PR01. The
safety of this dosing regimen was supported by data from Study 12-230, Study 201, Study
2PR01, Study 11-009, and Study 12-116. Of these studies, 12-230, 11-009 and 12-116 were
conducted by Memorial Sloan Kettering Cancer Centers (MSKCC), while 201 and 2PR01 were
conducted by the Applicant.
FDA recommends a dose cap for patients with a body weight (BW) > 50 kg, with a maximum
naxitamab dose of 450 mg/cycle. The dose cap is based on the pharmacokinetic characteristics
of naxitamab and the relationships between naxitamab exposure and efficacy/safety.
Naxitamab exposure is predicted to increase with increasing BW using per kg dosing. The
exposure-response relationship for safety suggests higher safety risks (bronchospasm,
hypotension, and hypertension) for patients with higher naxitamab exposures, while for
efficacy, no significant relationships between naxitamab exposure and ORR/complete response.
The exposure-response analysis for efficacy is based on the limited data from the trials and in
small number of patients with potential confounding factors (e.g., ADA effect).
The immunogenicity data from studies 12-230, 11-009 and 12-116 conducted by MSKCC are
deemed unreliable as the anti-drug-antibody (ADA) assays used in these trials were not
validated. Study 201, conducted by the Applicant, using validated ADA assays only tested 23
patients. Therefore, the following postmarket commitment (PMC) will be issued:
Conduct immunogenicity testing in all patients enrolled in the ongoing Study 201 and any
planned clinical trial(s) of naxitamab using the validated assays to adequately characterize the
incidences of anti-drug antibody (ADA) and neutralizing antibody against naxitamab. Assess
the impact of ADA on the pharmacokinetics (PK), safety and efficacy of naxitamab to inform
naxitamab labeling. Refer to the following FDA Guidance for Industry: “Immunogenicity
Assessment for Therapeutic Protein Products” available at
https://www.fda.gov/downloads/drugs/guidances/ucm338856.pdf.
68

Summary of Clinical Pharmacology Assessment
Pharmacology and Clinical Pharmacokinetics
The Applicant’s Position:
The PK data support the use of naxitamab for the treatment of refractory or relapsed high-risk
neuroblastoma in bone and/or bone marrow in combination with GM-CSF. The
pharmacokinetic evaluations are based on data from subjects treated with naxitamab for
high-risk neuroblastoma or other GD2-positive tumors within a clinical trial dose-escalation
setting. The pharmacokinetic properties of naxitamab have been characterized by data from
single doses and multiple doses across a range of dose regimens (including the proposed dose
regimen) as well as from population pharmacokinetic analyses. The analyses included doseexposure characterization and intrinsic factor evaluation. The proposed dosing regimen was
further supported by efficacy and safety evaluations.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s position that the PK data support the use of
naxitamab for the treatment of refractory or relapsed high-risk neuroblastoma in bone or bone
marrow, in combination with GM-CSF. Of note, only the PK data from Study 201 are deemed
reliable because the PK assays used in other studies, including studies 12-230, 11-009, and 12­
116, were not fully validated.
General Dosing and Therapeutic Individualization
6.2.2.1.

General Dosing

The Applicant’s Position:
The proposed dosing regimen was based on a clinical judgement during the dose-escalation
part of Trial 12-230 (see Section 8.1.1).
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position. Trial 12-230 evaluated naxitamab doses of 0.3,
0.6, 0.8, 1.0, 1.2, 1.4, 1.6, 1.8, 2.0, 2.2, 2.4, 2.6, 2.8, 3.0, 3.2 mg/kg QD administered on Day 1, 3,
and 5 of each 28-day cycle in the Phase 1 portion of the trial and evaluated naxitamab 3.0
mg/kg QD on Day 1, 3,and 5 of each 28-day cycle in the Phase 2 portion of the trial. Results of
this trial suggested that ORR was comparable across the different dose levels in Phase 1 and a
higher complete response (CR) rate was observed in the 3 mg/kg group from Phase 2 based on
cross-phase comparison. The MTD was not reached during the trial. However, a greater

6.2.2.2.

Therapeutic Individualization

The Applicant’s Position:
No therapeutic individualization is needed in the proposed indication based on demographic
factors and DDI considerations, as further detailed in Sections 6.3.2.3 and 6.3.2.4.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s position that no therapeutic individualization is
needed in the proposed indication based on DDI considerations. However, FDA recommends a
dose cap for BW >50 kg since naxitamab exposure is predicted to increase with increasing BW
using a per kg dosing regimen and the exposure-response relationship for safety suggests
higher safety risks (bronchospasm, hypotension, and hypertension) for patients with higher
naxitamab exposures.
6.2.2.3.

Outstanding Issues

The Applicant’s Position:
There are no outstanding issues.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA disagrees with the Applicant’s position of no outstanding clinical pharmacology issues
precluding approval of this application. FDA will issue a PMC requesting the Applicant to
adequately characterize naxitamab immunogenicity using fully-validated assays in the ongoing
Study 201 and any planned clinical trial(s) of naxitamab, and assess the impact of ADA on the
PK, safety and efficacy of naxitamab to inform naxitamab labeling.

Comprehensive Clinical Pharmacology Review
General Pharmacology and Pharmacokinetic Characteristics
The Applicant’s Position:
The PK of naxitamab has been evaluated in four clinical trials in patients. MSK evaluated the PK
of naxitamab in three trials (Trials 11-009, 12-116, and 12-230) as part of a dose-escalation
setting using a partially validated assay developed by MSK. Y-mAbs investigated the PK of
naxitamab with the proposed dose regimen in Trial 201 using a fully validated assay. In
addition, a population PK analysis of naxitamab was performed using data from these 4 trials to
characterize the PK after single and multiple doses in subjects with high-risk neuroblastoma and
to explore the impact of subject demographics and intrinsic factors on relevant PK parameters.
Naxitamab is administered by intravenous infusion only. There appeared to be a rapid
distribution phase during the first 48 hours after each infusion compared with the observed
terminal elimination phase after the third infusion. In the population PK analysis, the terminal
half-life was estimated to be 197 hours (8.2 days). Naxitamab exposure is therefore expected to
be comparable across cycles with 4 to 8 weeks between cycles. This is also in line with the
limited accumulation from one cycle to the next that has been observed in the precycle trough
concentration levels in Trial 201.
The dose-escalation trials (Trials 11-009, 12-116, and 12-230) generally showed that naxitamab
exposure (Cmax and AUC) increased approximately linearly with increasing dose within each trial.
The population PK analyses confirmed the dose proportionality seen in the individual trial
analyses.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s position on the general pharmacology and
pharmacokinetic characteristics of naxitamab. Of note, FDA calculated the terminal half-life of
naxitamab based on the PK data from Cycle 1 in Trial 201, as Trial 201 is the only trial that used
the fully validated assay in the naxitamab concentration measurement.

Clinical Pharmacology Questions
6.3.2.1 Does the clinical pharmacology program provide supportive evidence of
effectiveness?
The Applicant’s Position:
The clinical pharmacology program did not comprise specific supportive effectiveness
evaluations.
The FDA’s Assessment:
An exposure-response analysis for efficacy was conducted by the Applicant in response to FDA’s
information request. FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s position that no significant
relationships were identified between naxitamab exposure and ORR/CR, as detailed in Section
19.4.2. Of note, the exposure-response analysis examining the relationship between naxitamab
exposure and ORR/CR was conducted using limited data from the trials in a small number of
patients with potential confounding factors (e.g., ADA effect).
6.3.2.2 Is the proposed dosing regimen appropriate for the general patient
population for which the indication is being sought?
The Applicant’s Position:
The proposed dosing regimen has proven effective with manageable side effects in patients
with neuroblastoma, as detailed in Sections 8.1 and 8.2. Robustness of the results was further
evident from the subgroup analyses, as detailed in Sections 6.3.2.3 and 8.1.8.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s position. No significant relationships between
naxitamab exposure and ORR were identified; however, the exposure-response relationship for
safety suggests higher safety risks (Grade 2+ bronchospasm, hypotension, and hypertension)
for patients with higher naxitamab exposures, as detailed in Sections 19.4.2 and 19.4.3.
The Applicant proposed a naxitamab dosage of 3 mg/kg/day on Days 1, 3, and 5, for a total
dose of 9 mg/kg per cycle. Population PK analysis identified the effect of body weight on
naxitamab exposure with higher exposure at higher body weights. Naxitamab exposure was
simulated with the proposed dosing regimens across the relevant body weight range (10 –

Table 6-1. Summary of simulated naxitamab clearance and AUC0-672h versus bodyweight

Source: The Applicant’s population PK report (hu3F8-MS-02), Page 59, Table 11.
As shown in Table 6-1, mean predicted AUC0-672h is nearly doubled in patients with BW 70-80 kg
compared to patients with a BW of 20-30 kg. Flat dosing in adults and adolescents with higher
BW will minimize PK variability. There is limited clinical safety experience in patients with BW >
50 kg at naxitamab doses > 450 mg per cycle (i.e., 3 patients with BW >50 kg received a dose
>450 mg/cycle, with 2 patients receiving 3 infusions and 1 patient receiving 5 infusions).
Patients with BW >50 kg receiving 3 mg/kg dose are expected to have higher exposures than
that observed in the clinical trials. Therefore, based on exposure-matching, the recommended
dosage of naxitamab is 3 mg/kg/day up to 150 mg per day on Days 1, 3, and 5, for a total dose
of 9 mg/kg up to 450 mg per cycle.
6.3.2.3 Is an alternative dosing regimen or management strategy required for
subpopulations based on intrinsic patient factors?
The Applicant’s Position:
On the basis of the assessment of a potential impact of intrinsic factors on the PK of naxitamab,
no dose adjustment or change in dosing regimen is required to accommodate demographic
characteristics or special populations.
The effect of intrinsic factors such as body weight, body mass index, body surface area, lean
body mass, race, gender, age, and renal or hepatic function was evaluated in the population PK
analysis (Study hu3F8-MS-02). Only body weight was retained as a significant covariate, and
exposure was predicted to increase with increasing body weight. However, an assessment of
73
Version date: July 24, 2019 (ALL NDA/BLA reviews)
