NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761171}
{DANYELZA, naxitamab-gqgk}

3.0 mg/kg/day. The mean (SD) number of treatment cycles was 5.0 (4.33), ranging from 2 to 7
cycles. There were 12 subjects in the 2.6- to 3.0-mg/kg dose group, with a mean (SD) number of
treatment cycles of 2.8 (2.18), ranging from 1 to 8 cycles. Of these, 5 subjects were in the
3.0-mg/kg/day dose level.
Trial 12-116
In Trial 12-116, 12 subjects were exposed to naxitamab at doses ranging from 0.3 to
1.2 mg/kg/d). Mean (SD) number of treatment cycles were 2.0 (1.21), ranging from 1 to
4 cycles.
2PR01
In 2PR01, overall, 63 enrollments (52 patients) were exposed to naxitamab, with a mean (SD)
number of treatment cycles of 3.7 (2.62), ranging from 1 to 11 cycles. Of these 63 enrollments,
36 enrollments received naxitamab (9.0 mg/kg per cycle) in combination with GM-CSF as per
the Protocol 201/12-230 dose regimen.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally agrees with the description of exposure for Trials 12-230 and 201 above, and did
not confirm exposure for the subsequently listed supportive trials.
Notably, Trial 12-230 allowed multiple enrollments and the exposure data above reflects data
per enrollment. The median number of cycles initiated per enrollment in Trial 12-230 was 4.0
(range 1 – 15, as described above). When considering unique patients enrolled in Trial 12-230
(some of whom were enrolled multiple times), 46% of patients received 5 – 10 cycles, and 17%
received 11 or more cycles.
The median number of cycles initiated in Trial 201, which is an ongoing study, was 4 (range 1 –
8). Fifty-two percent of patients received < 5 cycles and 48% received 5 – 8 cycles as of the data
cutoff. In Study 201, among 25 patients who received DANYELZA in combination with GM-CSF,
12% were exposed for 6 months or longer and none were exposed for greater than one year.
The reviewer notes that the proposed product label suggests administration of treatment cycles
every 4 weeks until CR or PR, then 5 additional cycles. Subsequent optional cycles would be
(b) (4)
administered every 8 weeks
Therefore, a patient
receiving naxitamab would be expected to receive > 5 cycles of naxitamab, provided they did not
discontinue due to progressive disease or adverse event prematurely. Approximately half of the

Relevant characteristics of the safety population
The Applicant’s Position:
The clinical development program has evaluated naxitamab in patients ranging from 1 year of
age to 34 years of age, with the vast majority of subjects being pediatric patients 12 years of
age or younger. Trial subjects were of diverse ethnic backgrounds and represent a range of
baseline disease states, with a majority having refractory or relapsed neuroblastoma in bone
and/or bone marrow, thereby providing reassurance regarding the interpretation and the
validity of the safety conclusions. Overall, the demographics and baseline disease
characteristics of subjects treated in the clinical program reflect the intended target population.
A summary of demographics for the safety population in Trials 12-230, 201, and 2PR01 are
shown in Table 8-21.

Baseline disease characteristics
Of the 273 unique subjects in the safety population, 256 subjects were diagnosed with
neuroblastoma and 17 subjects were diagnosed with other GD2-positive malignancies (8 in Trial
11-009, 9 in Trial 12-116).

The majority of subjects treated in the clinical development program were diagnosed with
high-risk refractory or relapsed neuroblastoma in bone and/or bone marrow.
In the pivotal Trial 12-230, 31.3% of the subjects were diagnosed with an incomplete response
to salvage therapy prior to enrollment, and 18.3% had primary refractory disease. The
remaining subjects in the pivotal Trial 12-230 did not have evaluable disease and received
naxitamab as consolidation therapy and were as such not part of the efficacy population but
contribute to the safety population. The Trial 201 population includes 64% subjects with
primary refractory disease and 36% with an incomplete response to salvage therapy at
enrollment. Study 2PR01 included 8 categories of patients with neuroblastoma. Categories 1
and 2 (6 patients) comprised patients with primary refractory disease in bone and/or bone
marrow or relapsed disease with incomplete response to salvage treatment in bone and bone
marrow.

Adequacy of the safety database:
The Applicant’s Position:
The evaluation of safety is based on data from four clinical trials and a retrospective study with
data collected from subjects treated in a compassionate-use program and exposed to at least 1
dose of study treatment. Further details are provided in Section 8.2.
This population allows for an informed assessment of the safety profile of naxitamab in
combination with GM-CSF for the treatment of high-risk refractory or relapsed neuroblastoma
in bone and/or bone marrow.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA’s evaluation of safety is based primarily on the data from 117 unique patients enrolled in
Trial 12-230 and 25 unique patients enrolled in Trial 201. Analyses of data from other studies
were considered supportive.
Given the data collection in Trial 12-230 there are several limitations to the safety database.
Comprehensive collection of Grade 1 – 2 AEs was not required beyond Cycle 2 in this study. In
addition, action taken with the product in response to an adverse event was not captured in a

standard fashion. Dose discontinuations and modifications are described in the review to the
extent possible based on analyses of the safety database and safety narratives provided.
Adequacy of Applicant’s Clinical Safety Assessments
Issues Regarding Data Integrity and Submission Quality
The Applicant’s Position:
No meaningful concerns are anticipated in the quality and integrity of the submitted data sets
and individual case narratives.
The FDA’s Assessment:
See description of limitations of the database on the preceding page.
Categorization of Adverse Event
The Applicant’s Position:
The safety evaluation of naxitamab in combination with GM-CSF included the following:
• Treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs), defined as any AE with onset date from the
day of the first infusion of naxitamab up to and including 30 days after the last naxitamab
infusion
• Serious TEAEs, including deaths
• TEAEs leading to treatment discontinuation
• AEs of special interest (AESIs)
• Immunogenicity
• Clinical laboratory results
• Vital signs
• Safety in special groups (by age, body weight, gender, race, prior anti-GD2 treatment, and
ADA-status)
The Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities (MedDRA) Version 20.1 was used for coding of
AEs in Trials 12-230, 201, 11-009, and 12-116. MedDRA Version 21.1 was used for coding of AEs
in 2PR01. MedDRA Version 20.1 was used for coding of AEs for the ISS.
AEs were graded using the National Cancer Institute CTCAE (Version 4) in all trials.

Routine Clinical Tests
The Applicant’s Position:
In Trials 201, 12-230, 11-009, and 12-116, routine clinical laboratory data were collected and
evaluated as described in the protocols.
Under the 2PR01 retrospective study protocol, routine clinical laboratory data were not
collected.
The summaries included all laboratory assessments collected no later than 30 days after end of
naxitamab treatment.
The FDA’s Assessment:
Routine clinical tests in Trial 12-230 included:
• Chemistry including liver function testing (before each cycle and ~3 days after the last
dose of naxitamab for each cycle)
• Complete blood count on Day -4 prior to starting GM-CSF, and before GM-CSF on Days 1
– 5.
• ECG after dosing of naxitamab during Cycle 1 and then as clinically indicated
• Evaluation for anti-drug antibodies (ADA) before each cycle
• Tumor evaluation including bone marrow evaluation and imaging (CT, MRI, MIBG scan
or PET scan) before cycle 2, after cycle 4, and approximately every 3 months for 18
months from first treatment
• Pain assessment and vital signs prior to and during infusion of naxitamab and prior to
discharge from the day hospital
Routine clinical tests in Trial 201 included:

subsequent cycle.

1, 3 and 5.

platelet count >500,000/mm3, GM-CSF was held until values fall below the
abovementioned thresholds. CBC was repeated on sequential days for an

Serious Adverse Events
The Applicant’s Position:
Serious Adverse Events (SAEs)
In Trial 12-230, the percentage of subjects reporting at least one SAE was 41.0%. SAEs reported
in at least 5% of all SAF subjects included hypotension (11.1%), hypertension (10.3%), pyrexia
(9.4%), and device-related infection (6.8%).
In Trial 201, the percentage of subjects reporting at least one SAE was 32.0%. SAEs reported in
at least 5% of all subjects included anaphylactic reaction (12.0%) and pain (8.0%).
In 2PR01, the percentage of patients reporting at least one SAE was 32.7%. SAEs reported in at
least 5% of all patients included angioedema (5.8%).
Treatment-related SAEs
Treatment-related SAEs reported for Trial 12-230 SAF subjects, Trial 201 subjects, and patients
in 2PR01, is presented side-by-side in Table 8-24.
Treatment-related SAEs were reported for 29.1% of subjects in Trial 12-230 SAF. Treatmentrelated SAEs reported for 2 or more subjects included hypertension (10.3%), hypotension
(7.7%), pyrexia (5.1%), pain (1.7%), pyrexia (1.7%), vomiting (1.7%), anaphylactic reaction
(1.7%), and posterior reversible encephalopathy syndrome (PRES) (1.7%).
In Trial 201, treatment-related SAEs were reported for 20% of subjects. Treatment-related SAEs
reported for 2 or more subjects included anaphylactic reaction (12%).
In 2PR01, 17.3% unique patients reported at least one treatment-related SAE. Treatmentrelated SAEs reported for 2 or more subjects included bronchospasm (3.8%) and angioedema
(5.8%).

The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA performed an analysis of the incidence of SAEs in Trials 12-230 and 201, and performed a
selective review of SAEs in Trial 11-009, 12-115 and 2PR01. FDA did not agree with the
presentation of safety data noted above given the inclusion of only SAEs that were assessed as
related by the investigator and requested a revised table including all treatment-emergent
SAEs. Table 8-25 was provided by the Applicant and the data were confirmed by FDA.

Table 8-27: Selected Serious Adverse Event Narratives from Trials 12-203 and 201
Preferred Term/
Narrative and Reviewer Comments
Patient ID
Mental Status changes
Patient

(b) (6)

This is a 10-year-old male with stage 4 neuroblastoma enrolled in Study 12­
230 who experienced mental status changes two days after completion of
3rd cycle of naxitamab. The patient woke up crying, then fell back asleep and
was unarousable according to his mother. He alternated between being
unresponsive and being awake and screaming. Upon evaluations the
potential etiology was considered to be potentially related to use of
cannabidiol or seizures (although no seizure activity was observed on EEG).

Preferred Term/
Patient ID

Narrative and Reviewer Comments
He experienced mental status changes after Cycle 4 as well. Again, two days
after receipt of naxitamab, the patient was lethargic and difficult to arouse.
Upon arrival to urgent care, the patient was ‘dusky and unresponsive’ and
was placed on BiPap. He was treated for UTI, evaluated for seizures and for
meningitis/encephalitis. A workup for infectious causes of meningitis and
encephalitis was negative.
Reviewer comment: The pattern of two episodes of neurologic changes after
receipt of naxitamab is concerning. Of note, this patient had also received the
anti-GD2 antibody 8H9 at MSKCC and dinutuximab via a COG protocol. In
addition, in response to an information request regarding a subsequent event
of cardiac arrest (described later in this section), the investigator stated that
this patient had baseline neurologic deficits related to his disease,
confounding assessment of this event. Serious neurologic sequelae were not
reported for subsequent cycles of naxitamab.

Patient

(b) (6)

This is a 24-year-old male with high-risk neuroblastoma enrolled in Study 12­
230 who experienced Grade 3 aphasia following his 7th cycle of naxitamab.
Three days following administration of naxitamab he reported difficulty
speaking, headache and photophobia; he also reported difficulty spelling out
words and texting, and had 2 episodes of emesis. He was evaluated for PRES
and treated for hypertension. MRI did not demonstrate PRES and he was
treated with gabapentin for pain. The event was considered not related to
protocol therapy.
Reviewer comment: The Applicant assessed the event as not related to
naxitamab but an alternative etiology is not presented.

concern for hydromorphone overdose and Ativan out of concern for seizure
activity with some improvement in responsiveness after medication, and was
given fluid due to hypotension (87/49). Workup including brain imaging did

Reversible Posterior Leukoencephalopathy Syndrome (RPLS)/ Posterior Reversible Encephalopathy
Syndrome (PRES)
Patient

(b) (6)

This is a 7-year-old with Stage 4 neuroblastoma who experienced Grade 3
PRES two days following completion of naxitamab administration during
Cycle 1 of therapy on Study 12-203. One day after naxitamab administration
he developed nausea, emesis, headache, and transient bilateral vision loss
which was self-resolved. Two days after naxitamab administration, he
presented to a local hospital and was found to be hypertensive (150/100). He
was treated with antihypertensive medication, but developed a generalized
tonic-clonic seizure and was admitted to the pediatric intensive care unit
(PICU). He was intubated and EEG demonstrated subclinical status epilepticus
for approximately 2 hours. An MRI obtained the next day demonstrated
posterior reversible leukoencephaolopathy syndrome (RPLS). The patient
subsequently received one additional cycle of naxitamab before being
removed due to ADA.

Patient

(b) (6)

This is a 5 year old female with Stage 4 neuroblastoma who experienced
Grade 3 PRES following Cycle 1 of naxitamab on Study 12-203. Seven days
after completing naxitamab she presented to her local hospital with nausea,
headaches, abdominal pain, and was found to be hypertensive with altered
mental status and seizure activity. She was intubated, treated with
antiepileptics and antihypertensive medications. MRI confirmed RPLS/PRES.
The patient discontinued naxitamab due to the event.

230 who experienced Grade 3 apnea and hypoxia during his second dose of
naxitamab. Episodes of desaturation were self-resolving and occurred while
awake and asleep. He was treated with antibiotics based on new right lower
lobe opacity on chest x-ray and given oxygen by nasal cannula. Sleep study

Narrative and Reviewer Comments

Patient

This is an 11-year-old male with Stage 4 neuroblastoma enrolled in Study 12­
230 who experienced Grade 3 apnea and mental status changes following
Cycle 1 of naxitamab. He presented the day following completion of
naxitamab administration with continued pain as well as hypertension, and
was treated for pain and hypertension. Four days after completion of
naxitamab he returned to clinic with generalized pain and abdominal pain.
His mother also reported that he had been unresponsive for 3 - 4 seconds
when she tried to wake him. Upon arrival he had delayed capillary refill and
hypotension (60s/30s). During this hospitalization he had brief episodes of
apnea. He was treated presumptively for sepsis and evaluated for seizures.
The neurologic events were assessed as not related to protocol therapy.

(b) (6)

demonstrated central hypopnea requiring BiPAP while asleep. Patient
continued BiPAP overnight while on study.

Reviewer comment: The patient’s apparent episodes of apnea maybe related
to use of morphine for pain.
Acute Kidney Injury
Patient

(b) (6)

This is a 15-year-old female with Stage 4 neuroblastoma enrolled in Study 12­
230 who experienced Grade 4 acute kidney injury following Cycle 1 of
treatment with naxitamab. Two days after completion of the last dose of
Cycle 1 of naxitamab, the patient experienced nausea and vomiting and was
seen in urgent care. Two days later she was evaluated for constipation.
Laboratory studies demonstrated a Grade 3 creatinine elevation (4.5 mg/dl;
normal 0.6-1.3 mg/dl); her BUN was also increased (40 mg/dl; normal range
6-20 mg/dl). She began dialysis for acute kidney injury. During the admission,
she had a kidney biopsy which revealed a mild to moderate diffuse, acute
tubular injury, fairly diffuse active interstitial inflammation, and mild
glomerulomegaly with increased extracellular matrix. The patient's kidney
injury appeared to completely recover by the time of follow-up one month
later. The event was considered a dose-limiting toxicity and the patient was
removed from protocol therapy. (dose: 2.6 mg/kg/day)
Reviewer comment: The preceding history of nausea and vomiting could
indicate that the renal injury is largely pre-renal. Severe volume depletion can
precipitate acute tubular necrosis. The interstitial inflammation seen on
kidney biopsy could also indicate acute interstitial nephritis, which would
most likely be drug-related. The patient recovered following dialysis but as no
further naxitamab was given, recovery does not provide additional insight

This is a 5-year-old boy diagnosed with Stage 4 neuroblastoma
enrolled in Study 12-230 who developed Grade 2 respiratory distress
during Cycle 6 of naxitamab. He received blow-by oxygen and

was evaluated in an urgent care center the day following Dose 2 due to
wheezing and oxygen desaturation with visible work of breathing. He
received nebulizers, hydroxyzine, and Singulair. A respiratory viral
panel was negative. His 3rd dose of naxitamab for that cycle was held,
and he was treated with albuterol, Singulair and Claritin. He was
admitted overnight and the event was reported as resolved the next
day. He received the subsequent cycle of naxitamab prior to
discontinuing due to completion of therapy (the patient demonstrated
a complete response).

(b) (6)

Reviewer comment: The narrative demonstrates that respiratory
supportive care was needed during infusion and subsequently recurred
after infusion. The need for respiratory support during infusion
suggests that this was an infusion reaction.
This is a 4-year-old year-old with Stage 4 neuroblastoma enrolled in
Study 12-230 who developed Grade 3 pneumonia, 12 days after his 1st
dose of naxitamab. Later, during his second cycle he presented again
with wheezing prior to Dose 2 of naxitamab, and developed a fever
post-dose with hypoxia and stridor. He was treated with racemic
epinephrine, albuterol, and started on antibiotics for left lower lobe
opacity.
Reviewer comment: The event of pneumonia does not appear to be
related to naxitamab, but the respiratory distress during the patient’s
second cycle likely represents a component of bronchospasm related to
naxitamab on top of underlying pneumonia.

Patient ID

Narrative and Reviewer Comments

Patient

This is a 10-year-old male with Stage 4 neuroblastoma enrolled in
Study 12-230 who experienced a cardiac arrest following Cycle 6 dose
1 of naxitamab. Following completion of the infusion and after his last
narcotic rescue (approximately 1.5 hours from the time of naxitamab
infusion completion and hydromorphone completion), patient fainted
and became cyanotic and unresponsive. He received two minutes of
CPR and IM epinephrine. He was monitored overnight in the PICU. The
event was assessed as not related to protocol therapy.

Other

(b) (6)

Reviewer note: In response to an information request the investigator
provided rationale for stating the event was not related to naxitamab.
This patient had received >5 years of aggressive multimodality therapy
including intrathecal and radiation treatments for three relapses in the
brain, with residual central and peripheral neurological defects
including central and obstructive sleep apnea. He often experienced
periods of unresponsiveness apparently unrelated to any oncologic
treatment which necessitated emergency transport.
Given the clinical history of this patient, this reviewer agrees that this
event may be unrelated to naxitamab; however, as cardiac arrest and
sudden death have been rarely reported following administration of
dinutuximab, the reviewer feels a contribution of naxitamab cannot be
entirely excluded.
Source: FDA primary clinical review, CSRs for Trial 12-230 and 201
One additional serious adverse event in a patient receiving naxitamab was submitted to IND
132793 but was not included in the BLA safety population. This patient received naxitamab in
combination with GM-CSF according to the MSK study entitled “16-1643: Naxitamab (Hu3F8)

insufficiency are not well described in the narrative, which indicates “despite several attempts,
no further information provided.” The Applicant has included transverse myelitis as a warning in
Section 5 of the label, and this reviewer agrees that it should be included.

The narratives submitted in the 120-day safety update did not suggest new safety signals

Dropouts and/or Discontinuations Due to Adverse Effects
The Applicant’s Position:
Discontinuations due to AEs were few. In Trial 12-230 Phase 1, 4 subjects discontinued due to
dose-limiting toxicity (DLT): anaphylactic reaction, acute kidney injury, altered mental status,
and hypertension; and 3 subjects discontinued due to AEs: anaphylactic reaction, angioedema,
and hypotension. In Trial 12-230 Phase 2, three subjects discontinued due to hypertension, one
subject due to PRES, and one subject due to excessive toxicity, where no specific AEs, but a
number of nonserious AEs could be linked to the discontinuation. In Trial 201, two subjects
discontinued due to anaphylactic reaction, and one subject due to respiratory depression. In
2PR01, four patients discontinued due to AEs: anaphylactic shock, neuralgia, apnoea
(nonserious), and tumor haemorrhage (not related to naxitamab).
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the description of discontinuations due to adverse events in Trial 201.
Action taken with naxitamab in response to specific adverse events was not reliably captured
during Trial 12-230 and this information was not included as a standard variable in the adverse
event dataset. The Applicant conducted a review of narratives and identified adverse events
that prompted discontinuation; FDA’s manual review of the narratives also yielded 8 patients
who discontinued due to excessive toxicity and four patients who experienced an AE leading to
discontinuation, totaling 12 patients (10%) in among all patients (n=117) in Trial 12-230 who
discontinued naxitamab due to treatment-emergent toxicity as detailed in Table 8-30. This is
consistent with the Applicant’s description above. Six of these patients (8.3%) received the
recommended phase 2 dose of naxitamab and were included in FDA’s primary safety analysis
for Trial 12-230 (n=72).

Dose Interruption/Reduction Due to Adverse Effects
The Applicant’s Position:
Serious infusion reactions requiring urgent interventions have occurred during naxitamab
infusions. These reactions may be mitigated by premedications, supportive medications,
infusion interruption, infusion rate reduction, or permanent discontinuation of naxitamab as
described in naxitamab product information.

Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events and Adverse Reactions
The Applicant’s Position:
Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events (TEAEs)
In Trial 12-230, the percentage of subjects reporting at least one TEAE was 99.1% in SAF and
97.7% in PAS. TEAEs occurring in at least 50% of SAF subjects included pain (98.3%),
hypotension (82.9%), abdominal pain (81.2%), urticaria (79.5%), pyrexia (74.4%), vomiting
(61.5%), pain in extremity (60.7%), cough (60.7%), pruritus (59.0%), flushing (55.6%), nausea

(57.3%), and decreased appetite (52.1%), as well as the laboratory-parameter–associated TEAEs
of anaemia (71.8%), lymphocyte count decreased (67.5%), platelet count decreased (65.0%),
hypoalbuminaemia (58.1%), hyperglycaemia (53.0%), WBC count decreased (53.0%), and
neutrophil count decreased (50.4%). For Trial 11-009 and Trial 12-116, the most common TEAEs
reported for subjects were overall similar to those reported for Trial 12-230 SAF subjects.
In Trial 201, the percentage of subjects reporting at least one TEAE was 100%. TEAEs occurring
in at least 50% of subjects included pain (100%), hypotension (100%), urticaria (80.0%),
bronchospasm (76.0%), tachycardia (76.0%), pyrexia (72.0%), cough (60.0%), vomiting (60.0%),
diarrhoea (56.0%), and nausea (56.0%).
In 2PR01, the percentage of patients reporting at least one TEAE was 100%. TEAEs occurring in
at least 50% of patients included pain (100%), hypotension (86.5%), tachycardia (82.7%),
urticaria (69.2%), bronchospasm (57.7%), and vomiting (53.8%).
Adverse Reactions (Treatment-related TEAEs)
Trial 12-230
In 12-230 SAF subjects, 98.3% reported at least one treatment-related TEAE (see Table 8-33).
Treatment-related TEAEs reported for at least 50% of subjects included pain (98.3%),
hypotension (82.9%), abdominal pain (80.3%), urticaria (77.8%), pyrexia (71.8%), vomiting
(58.1%), pruritus (56.4%), pain in extremity (54.7%), nausea (53.0%), and flushing (53.0%). More
than three-fourths of the treatment-related TEAEs reported in the SAF were infusion-related
(77.9%). Pain accounted for the largest proportion of infusion-related TEAEs (20.2%) and was
reported for almost all Trial 12-230 SAF subjects (98.3%). Other commonly reported infusionrelated TEAEs for subjects in SAF group included hypotension (81.2%), abdominal pain (76.1%),
urticaria (76.1%), pyrexia (59.8%), vomiting (54.7%), and flushing (53.0%).
Pain was also reported by other terms such as noncardiac chest pain, facial pain, chest pain,
abdominal pain, abdominal pain upper, oral pain, oropharyngeal pain, pain in extremity, back
pain, neck pain, musculoskeletal pain, bone pain, musculoskeletal chest pain, pain in jaw,
urinary tract pain, penile pain, vulvovaginal pain, genital pain, scrotal pain, testicular pain, ear
pain, and lymph node pain. The pool of these terms account for about 28.5% of all treatmentrelated TEAEs reported for subjects in the SAF.
When evaluated by a pain score, the highest scores for pain were reported during the
30-minute naxitamab infusion, with pain subsiding before hospital discharge.
The reported treatment-related TEAEs of peripheral sensory neuropathy, paraesthesia,
peripheral motor neuropathy, and neuralgia, were all nonserious Grades 1 or 2.

Trial 201
All Trial 201 subjects (25 [100%]) experienced at least one TEAE considered to be related to
naxitamab treatment (see Table 8-34). The most commonly reported related TEAEs were
known reactions to infusion of naxitamab. These included pain (100%), hypotension (100%),
urticaria (80.0%), bronchospasm (76.0%), tachycardia (76.0%), and pyrexia (64%); five of these
were also reported as treatment-related TEAEs for at least 50% of Trial 12-230 SAF subjects
(i.e., pain, hypotension, pyrexia, urticaria, and cough). The reported treatment-related TEAEs of
peripheral sensory neuropathy, paraesthesia, and neuralgia, were all nonserious Grades 1 or 2.

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761171}
{DANYELZA, naxitamab-gqgk}

2PR01
In 2PR01, all patients (52) experienced at least one TEAE considered related to naxitamab. All
patients had at least one naxitamab-related event of pain in their medical records. Other
commonly collected naxitamab-related TEAEs were hypotension (86.5%), tachycardia (82.7%),
urticaria (69.2%), bronchospasm (57.7%), and vomiting (50.0%). Pain, hypotension, urticaria,
and vomiting were also reported as treatment-related TEAEs for at least 50% of subjects in Trial
12-230 SAF.
Grade 3 and Higher TEAEs
In Trial 12-230 SAF, the percentage of subjects reporting at least one Grade 3+ TEAE was 78.6%.
Grade 3+ TEAEs reported for at least 10% of all subjects in the SAF included stridor (13.7%),
hypoxia (11.1%), as well as laboratory-parameter–associated TEAEs of lymphocyte count
decreased (40.2%), neutrophil count decreased (27.4%), platelet count decreased (24.8%),
aneamia (23.1%), WBC decreased (20.5%), , and hypokalaemia (19.7%). For subjects in Trials 11­
009 and 12-116, the most common TEAEs reported at Grade 3+ were also reported for a similar
percentage as for the subjects in Trial 12-230 SAF.
In Trial 201, the percentage of subjects reporting at least one Grade 3+ TEAE was 92%. Grade 3+
TEAEs reported by at least 10% of subjects included pain (72.0%), hypotension (60.0%), urticaria
(40.0%), bronchospasm (28.0%), depressed level of consciousness (16.0%), anaphylactic
reaction (12.0%), and abdominal pain (12.0%).
In 2PR01, the percentage of patients reporting at least one Grade 3+ TEAE was 100%. Grade 3+
TEAEs reported by at least 10% of patients included pain (96.2%), hypotension (65.4%), urticaria
(48.1%), bronchospasm (21.2%), and rash (21.2%).
Grade 3 and higher Adverse Reactions (Treatment-related TEAEs)
In Trial 12-230 SAF, treatment-related Grade 3+ TEAEs reported for two or more subjects
included stridor (13.7%), hypoxia (8.5%), hypotension (5.1%), hypertension (5.1%), urticaria
(4.3%), diarrhoea (2.6%), vomiting (1.7%), pyrexia (1.7%), anaphylactic reaction (1.7%), and
PRES (1.7%), as well as laboratory-parameter-associated TEAEs of platelet count decreased
(16.2%), WBC count decreased (15.4%), alanine aminotransferase increased (3.4%), aspartate
aminotransferase increased (2.6%), neutrophil count decreased (1.7%).
In Trial 201, treatment-related Grade 3+ TEAEs reported for two or more subjects included pain
(72.0%), hypotension (60.0%), urticaria (40.0%), bronchospasm (28.0%), abdominal pain (12%),
anaphylactic reaction (12%), stridor (8%), and headache (8%).

Version date: July 24, 2019 (ALL NDA/BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant
and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 4707829

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761171}
{DANYELZA, naxitamab-gqgk}

In 2PR01, treatment-related TEAEs reported for two or more patients included pain (96.2%),
hypotension (65.4%), urticaria (48.1%), bronchospasm (21.2%), rash (21.2%), hypoxia (7.7%),
abdominal pain (7.7%), hypertension (5.8%), angioedema (5.8%), anaphylactic reaction (5.8%),
and anaphylactic shock (3.8%).
Conclusion/Summary
Common treatment-related events were mainly Grades 1 or 2 and included pain (including
pain, abdominal pain, and pain in extremity), pyrexia, urticaria, pruritus, nausea, vomiting,
hypotension, hypertension, and bronchospasm. More than 75% of treatment-related events
were infusion-related. Generally, the highest scores for pain were reported during the
30-minute naxitamab infusion, with pain subsiding before hospital discharge.
Grade 3+ treatment-related events of importance included anaphylaxis, angioedema,
hypotension, bronchospasm, and hypertension. The incidence of treatment-related SAEs across
trials was consistent and below 30%. No fatal events were reported.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA’s primary analysis of treatment-emergent adverse events focused on the population of
patients who received the recommended phase 2 dose of naxitamab in Trial 12-230 and all
patients in Trial 201. In a single arm trial, FDA considers all treatment-emergent adverse events
in its analysis and cannot confirm the analysis of treatment relatedness nor the discussion of
“adverse reactions” provided by the Applicant above. Tables including all treatment-emergent
adverse events were provided by the Applicant below. FDA edited Table 8-35 by removing
TEAEs with an incidence of < 10% and laboratory-based TEAEs in the investigations SOC, as
these will be evaluated based on the ADLB dataset. In general FDA’s analysis of non-laboratory
related TEAEs was in agreement with that produced by the sponsor with some minor
discrepancies generally consisting of only 1 – 2 patients. FDA added columns which include the
calculation based on FDA’s assessment, where discrepant.
The tables below provide analyses by preferred term with no pooling of related terms. For an
analysis of adverse events with pooled terms, refer to the discussion of submission-specific
safety issues in section 8.2.5 and to the final product labeling.

{DANYELZA, naxitamab-gqgk}

Laboratory Findings
The Applicant’s Position:
Clinical Chemistry
Renal function
Overall, increased levels of serum creatine were infrequently seen. Only four SAEs on renal
function were reported (three acute kidney injury and one renal failure). Two SAEs were not
related to naxitamab treatment, and in the other two, confounding factors were present: in
one, preceding severe nausea and vomiting occurred, and in the other, 3 drugs were
co-suspected (naxitamab, GM-CSF, and antibiotics).
Liver function
Abnormal laboratory values associated with liver function were expected in subject populations
for the naxitamab trials, given the nature of their disease, prior exposure to anticancer therapy,
and wide inclusion/exclusion criteria.
No significant trends were observed across cycles for clinical chemistry parameters associated
with liver function tests and there was no indication of drug-induced liver injury (DILI).
Hematology
Abnormal hematology values were expected in subject populations for the naxitamab trials,
given the nature of their disease, prior exposure to anticancer therapy, wide inclusion/exclusion
criteria, allowed transfusions, and bone marrow stimulations prior to inclusion.
There was no sign of myelotoxicities despite the high number of hematological laboratory
TEAEs reported in Trial 12-230. The only observations that seemed to be linked to treatment
were the leukocytes and neutrophil counts fluctuating to various degrees during cycles due to
GM-CSF treatment. Of SAEs reported across all trials, one febrile neutropenia was related, one
SAE neutrophil count decreased related to GM-CSF, two platelet count decreased SAEs were
not related, and three SAEs of lymphocyte count decreased were not related.

FDA does not agree that significant myelosuppression was not observed in the trial. As
demonstrated in the tables above, severe (Grade 3 – 4) thrombocytopenia (40% of patients in
Trial 12-230 and 17 % of patients in Trial 201) and neutropenia (46% of patients in Trial 12-230
and 39% of patients in Trial 201) were not uncommon. FDA agrees that the population of
patients enrolled in these trials has been heavily pre-treated, but notes that the tables above
include patients with worsening laboratory parameters from baseline.
Based on an analysis performed by the Applicant in Trial 12-230, events of Grade 4
thrombocytopenia lasted 0 – 30 days (median 2 days) and events of Grade 4 neutropenia lasted
0 – 10 days (median 2 days).

{DANYELZA, naxitamab-gqgk}

Some electrolyte perturbations were also observed, with 32% of patients in Trial 12-230 and 8%
of patients in Trial 201 experiencing Grade 3 – 4 hypokalemia. Calcium, magnesium and
phosphorous were not routinely assessed in Trial 201 and are not included in the table nor in
the dataset.
Vital Signs
The Applicant’s Position:
Overall, variations in both diastolic blood pressure and systolic blood pressure around
naxitamab dosing days were seen, which was anticipated, considering that hypotension is a
known infusion-related reaction.
The FDA’s Assessment:
Given the differences in age-based norms for individual patients, individual adverse event data
for blood pressure (hypotension, hypertension, etc.) provide greater clinical relevance than
average data across patients. See discussion of hypertension and hypotension in Section 8.2.5.
Two patients in Trial 12-230 and no patients in Trial 201 experienced events of sinus
bradycardia that were reported as AEs; events in both patients occurred on the day of infusion
and were assessed as definitely related to naxitamab. Forty-six patients (39.3%) experienced
sinus tachycardia (1.7% Grade 3) in Trial 12-230. The majority of events occurred on the day of
infusion and were assessed as related to naxitamab. Twenty-one (84%) patients experienced
events of tachycardia or sinus tachycardia in Trial 201, with one patient (4%) experiencing a
Grade 3 event. Almost all events occurred on the day of a naxitamab infusion.
Reviewer note: Given the prevalence of pain during infusion of naxitamab, the occurrence of
tachycardia may be due to pain. Tachycardia may also represent physiologic response to
hypotension, which was also experienced during infusions, or as part of hypersensitivity reaction
such as anaphylaxis.
Electrocardiograms (ECGs)
The Applicant’s Position:
Abnormal ECG findings were observed in Trial 12-230 and Trial 201. In Trial 201, the clinical
significance of ECG findings was recorded. None of the observed abnormalities were considered
clinically significant by Investigator.

One patient in Trial 201 had an unspecified arrhythmia (Grade 2) other than tachycardia
recorded as an adverse event, assessed as related and reported as resolved. FDA requested
further information regarding this case, described as occasional bradycardia (heart rate as low
as 32 – 38 beats per minute) with fast and spontaneous recovery to > 120 beats per minute).
This event appears to be isolated and self-resolving.
The FDA’s Assessment:
In Trial 12-230, ECGs were planned to be conducted only during Phase 1; the dataset provided a
narrative description or result for abnormal ECGs. Fifty-six abnormal ECGs were identified,
primarily describing sinus tachycardia. Patients who experienced prolonged QT or borderline
prolonged QT are described below; confounding medications are noted where applicable.

CTCAE v 4.03 defines Grade 3 electrocardiogram QT corrected interval prolonged as QTc of
501ms or greater on two separate ECGs. Although two patients had measurements of 501
ms or greater, it is not clear that these were confirmed with repeat measurement.
There were no reported adverse events of QT prolongation. In Trial 201, the datasets
indicated that three patients had abnormal ECGs that were not clinically significant. Details
regarding these abnormalities were requested by FDA and findings did not include any
arrhythmias or QT prolongation. Datasets did not include details such as QT interval as
actual ECG measurements were not captured on trial according to the protocol. FDA
considered that, due to the low likelihood of direct ion channel interaction, the likelihood of
a monoclonal antibody causing QT prolongation is low.

No QT trials were conducted.

FDA has no additional comments. See description of ECG findings above.

infusion to first positive ADA titer was similar in Trials 201 and 12-230 (20.5 and 19.0 days,
respectively), ranging from 12 to 64 days.

The FDA’s Assessment:
Please see clinical pharmacology assessment of the impact of ADA in Section 6.3.2 and Section
19.4. The assessment is limited by the lack of a fully validated assay in Trial 12-230 and only two
patients with positive ADA post-baseline in Trial 201.
Clinical Outcome Assessment (COA) Analyses Informing
Safety/Tolerability
The Applicant’s Position:
See Section 8.1.2, Trial results.
The FDA’s Assessment:
Assessments of pain, which occurred in nearly all patients who received naxitamab in the safety
population considered for the BLA, were performed in accordance with standard medical
practice and did not include any additional patient-reported outcome assessments. Analyses of
events of pain are described in Sections 8.2.4 and 8.2.5.
Safety Analyses by Demographic Subgroups
The Applicant’s Position:
In the population PK model, naxitamab exposure was predicted to increase with body weight.
In the safety data available, there were no signs of increasing toxicity with increasing body
weight.
Age was not a statistically significant covariate in the population PK model. In the age Group 2
to less than 6 years; infusion-related TEAEs of pyrexia, hypotension, bronchospasm, and stridor
appeared more frequent. In the age Group 2 to less than 12 years, cough and wheezing
appeared more frequently; however, children are known to differ in disease presentation by
age.
Safety data do not indicate differences in safety between gender or race.

The FDA’s Assessment:
The Applicant provided the following tables describing the safety experience by demographic
subgroups. FDA confirmed these tables, though numbers differed slightly in the analysis of Trial
12-230. In both trials, it appeared males were more likely to have a Grade 3 or higher TEAE but
experienced fewer TEAEs leading to discontinuation. However, given the extremely limited
number of patients in the safety population, the differences may be due to chance and extreme
caution should be used in interpreting any differences between groups. There did not appear to
be significant differences in adverse event trends by age or by race and all analyses are limited
by the small number of patients in each of the subgroups.

Analysis of Submission-Specific Safety Issues
The Applicant’s Position:
AESIs included hypotension and bronchospasm occurring on the same day as a naxitamab
infusion and hypertension, and PRES starting at any time.
Bronchospasm
In Trial 12-230, treatment-related bronchospasm was reported for 19.7% of SAF subjects. All
TEAEs of bronchospasm reported for SAF subjects occurred on the same day as a naxitamab
infusion. No Grade 3+ TEAEs of bronchospasm were reported for Trial 12-230 subjects, and no
TEAEs of bronchospasm were determined to be serious. In Trials 11-009 and 12-116, treatmentrelated TEAEs of bronchospasm at any grade were reported for a smaller percentage of subjects
than for Trial 12-230 SAF subjects. Grade 3+ TEAEs of bronchospasm and serious events of
bronchospasm were not reported for any subjects in Trial 11-009 or Trial 12-116.
In Trial 201, treatment-related TEAEs of bronchospasm was reported for 76.0% of subjects.
Treatment-related bronchospasm was reported at Grade 3+ severity for 28.0% of subjects in
Trial 201. No TEAEs of bronchospasm reported for Trial 201 subjects were considered serious.
In 2PR01, treatment-related TEAEs of bronchospasm were captured for 57.7% of 2PR01
patients. Grade 3+ treatment-related TEAEs of bronchospasm were captured for 21.2% of
213
Version date: July 24, 2019 (ALL NDA/BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant
and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 4707829

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761171}
{DANYELZA, naxitamab-gqgk}

2PR01 patients. Two treatment-related TEAEs of bronchospasm reported (3.8% of patients)
were considered serious.
Hypotension
In Trial 12-230, treatment-related hypotension was reported for 82.9% of SAF subjects.
Infusion-related hypotension was reported for 81.2% of subjects in the SAF. Although TEAEs of
hypotension at any grade were frequently reported in Trial 12-230, Grade 3+ and serious events
of hypotension were infrequently reported. In Trials 11-009 and 12-116, Grade 3+ TEAEs of
hypotension and serious events of hypotension were reported for similar percentages of
subjects in Trials 11-009 and 12-116 compared with Trial 12-230 SAF subjects.
In Trial 201, TEAEs of hypotension were reported for all subjects, and all TEAEs of hypotension
were naxitamab related. Grade 3+ treatment-related TEAEs of hypotension were reported for
60.0% of subjects. No SAEs of hypotension were reported for Trial 201 subjects.
In 2PR01, TEAEs of hypotension were reported for 86.5% of patients, and all but one TEAE of
hypotension were naxitamab-related. Grade 3+ TEAEs of hypotension were collected for 65.4%
of 2PR01 patients. All of the Grade 3+ TEAEs of hypotension were naxitamab-related. No TEAEs
of hypotension reported for 2PR01 patients were considered serious.
Hypertension
In Trial 12-230, hypertension was reported for 19.7% of SAF subjects. Treatment-related
hypertension was reported for 17.1% of SAF subjects. For treatment-related hypertension
occurring at the day of naxitamab infusion, 12.0% of SAF subjects reported infusion-related
hypertension. Grade 3+ TEAEs of hypertension were infrequent in Trial 12-230, where Grade 3+
TEAEs were reported for 5.1% of SAF subjects and infusion-related TEAEs were reported for
2.6% of SAF subjects. About 30% of TEAEs of hypertension were considered serious. About half
of the serious hypertension events started on the day of or 1 calendar day after Cycle 1,
Infusion 3, and other serious hypertension started at earlier infusions in Cycle 1. In Trial 11-009
subjects, hypertension was reported for a similar or a slightly smaller percentage than for all
subjects in Trial 12-230 SAF. In Trial 12-116, no TEAEs of hypertension were reported.
In Trial 201, treatment-related TEAEs of hypertension were reported for 44.4% of subjects. Only
one Grade 3+ treatment-related TEAE of hypertension was reported for one subject (4.0%) in
Trial 201. No TEAEs of hypertension reported for Trial 201 subjects were considered serious.
In 2PR01, treatment-related TEAEs of hypertension were captured for 28.8% of patients.
Grade 3+ treatment-related TEAEs of hypertension were captured for 5.8% of 2PR01 patients.

Version date: July 24, 2019 (ALL NDA/BLA reviews)

and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 4707829

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761171}
{DANYELZA, naxitamab-gqgk}

No TEAEs of hypertension reported for 2PR01 patients were considered serious.
Posterior Reversible Encephalopathy Syndrome
In Trial 12-230, PRES was reported for two Phase 2 subjects. Both TEAEs of PRES were Grade 3
in severity, serious, and related to naxitamab. Neither of the PRES TEAEs was infusion-related,
with both events starting 2 to 7 days after Cycle 1, Infusion 3. One subject was discontinued
from trial because of PRES, and the other subject continued in the trial. PRES was not reported
for Trial 201 subjects, Trial 11-009 subjects, Trial 12-116 subjects, or 2PR01 patients.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally agrees with the discussion above regarding adverse events of special interest. In
addition to the events, above, FDA considered neurologic events including pain, neuropathy,
and neurological disorders of the eye as events of special interest given the mechanism of
action of naxitamab.
Note that the Applicant uses the term “infusion-related” to indicate events which occurred on
the same day as an infusion of naxitamab. In some cases the Applicant has reported
“treatment-related” events, whereas FDA considers all treatment-emergent events in its
analysis. In cases where the incidence of an adverse event differs in the FDA analysis based on
presentation of only related events, or for other reasons, the incidence will be described below.
FDA considered anaphylaxis and angioedema, bronchospasm potentially associated with
hypoxia, stridor, and hypotension as components of infusion-related reactions or
hypersensitivity, which may be clinically indistinguishable.
Infusion-Related Reactions
Infusion-related reactions were very common in patients in the safety population. The
Applicant did not provide a pooled term definition of infusion-related reactions, but described
manifestations of infusion reactions separately. FDA performed a pooled term analysis
including the following terms: hypotension, bronchospasm, flushing, wheezing, stridor,
urticaria, dyspnea, pyrexia, infusion related reaction, face edema, swelling face, orbital edema,
periorbital edema, lip edema, lip swelling, mouth edema, tongue edema, swollen tongue,
mouth swelling, angioedema, respiratory tract edema, chills, hypoxia, pruritis, rash macular,
rash maculo-papular, rash erythematous, rash, and respiratory distress. The analysis was
restricted to events which occurred the day of the infusion of naxitamab or the day following an
infusion. The intent of the analysis is to capture events within 24 hours; because of the limited
number of events across studies which had recorded adverse event start times, the analysis
215
Version date: July 24, 2019 (ALL NDA/BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant
and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 4707829

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761171}
{DANYELZA, naxitamab-gqgk}

included events which occurred at any time on the day following the infusion. In Trial 12-230,
among patients who received the RP2D (n=72), 94% of patients experienced an infusion
reaction based on this analysis with 32% experiencing a Grade 3 – 4 infusion reaction; of these
there was one Grade 4 event of hypotension (1.4%). Infusion reactions occurred in Cycles 1 – 7
(median cycle 1). Serious events (n=12) included hypotension and pyrexia, and occurred during
Cycles 1 – 4. Grade 3 – 4 events (n=34) occurred during Cycles 1 – 7 and 56% of events occurred
during Cycle 1. Six patients (8.3%) had an infusion interrupted for an infusion-related reaction
based on the retrospective review described earlier in the review.
In Trial 201, 100% of patients experienced an event meeting this definition and 68% of patients
experienced a Grade 3 event; there were no Grade 4 infusion reactions as defined by the
pooled term. The incidence of components of infusion-related reactions will be described
separately below. Any Grade infusion reaction occurred in cycles 1 – 8 (median cycle 3), and
occurred with any dose of naxitamab during a cycle (1 – 3); 48% of events occurred with the
first dose of naxitamab of a cycle. Grade 3 events (n=128) occurred during cycles 1 – 7 (median
cycle 2). Thirty-three percent of Grade 3 events occurred during cycle 1 and 58% occurred
during the first infusion of a cycle. Twenty patients (80%) had an infusion interrupted based on
an infusion-related reaction; 80% of patients required an infusion rate decrease for an event of
infusion-related reaction. Approximately 50% of events had a the time of onset of infusion
reaction recorded. Of these events, the median time to onset was 0.34 hours or 20 minutes
following initiation of the infusion(range: 1 hour post initiation of the infusion to 16.5 hours
after the infusion). Eighty-five percent of infusion reactions with recorded start and end times
started within 30 minutes of initiation of infusion.
Reviewer note: Two events were reported with a start time prior to the initiation of
infusion of naxitamab, and including pyrexia and urticaria. One is assessed as related to
naxitamab This reviewer suggests that an error in recording start time of either AE or
infusion may explain the occurrence of an AE assessed as related to naxitamab occurring
before the initiation of the infusion.
The Applicant provided a review of serious grouped-term infusion reactions and evaluated for
patients who required ICU admission. One patient in Trial 12-230 was reported as requiring
vasopressor support for an infusion reaction. Per review of the narratives, several patients
required admission to the intensive care unit (PICU) for monitoring. Seven patients with terms
meeting this pooled term definition of infusion reactions were admitted to the ICU although
only three were assessed as probably related to naxitamab. Other events were suspicious for
infection or postoperative hypotension; this reviewer agrees with this assessment based on the
narratives.

and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761171}

One patient with Grade 4 hypotension required use of vasopressor support; although this event
occurred within the time frame evaluated for infusion related reactions, the event occurred the
day following the infusion and the narrative suggests that this event was consistent with sepsis,
supported by a positive blood culture (Staphylococcus aureus).
According to the Applicant’s analysis, 92% of patients with an event of infusion-related reaction
(grouped term) required concomitant fluid administration. The analysis in Trial 12-230 was
limited by incomplete information on concomitant medications for patients outside the primary
efficacy analysis set and included 14.6% of 41 patients who received fluid for a TEAE of infusionrelated reaction.
Pre-medication administered to patients in the safety population was variable. In Trial 12-230,
the protocol suggested premedication with analgesics and antihistamines but did not require
pre-medication. The protocol for Trial 201 contained specific instructions including the
following:
-

-

Gabapentin and oxycodone for pain, with IV opioids as needed
Corticosteroids for all patients at the first infusion in the first cycle and in the following
circumstances:
o At the first infusion in the following cycle if the patients had a Grade 3
bronchospasm/anaphylaxis at the last cycles first infusion
o At the following naxitamab infusion if the patients had a Grade 3
bronchospasm/anaphylaxis at the last infusion
o At the investigator’s discretion when infusion related reactions are not satisfiable
controlled by the other premedications.
Antipyretics (acetaminophen/paracetamol)
Antihistamines
H2 antagonist
Antiemetics
Anxiolytics, if applicable

The requirement for administration of corticosteroids for all patients prior to the first dose was
implemented soon before the data cut-off for the NDA and is not reflected in the
premedication that was actually administered in the safety analysis set for Trial 201. While
100% of patients received antihistamines and antipyretics, and 91 – 100% received H2
antagonists for all infusions in Cycle 1 (n=23 – 25), only 24% of patients received
corticosteroids for infusion 1 of cycle 1. The Applicant stated that the change in the protocol
was implemented given the high rate of bronchospasm observed in this trial (76% all Grades,
28% Grade 3 -4).

In Trial 12-230, collection of concomitant medication data was limited to patients in the
Applicant’s efficacy analysis set (n=45); among these patients, only one patient received
corticosteroids prior to the first dose in Cycle 1 and only one other corticosteroid pre­
medication administration (for infusion 3 of Cycle 2) is recorded for any patient. However,

(infusions 1 – 3, Cycles 1 – 13) ranged from 80 – 100%.
Reviewer note: Although the safety dataset does not reflect universal administration of
corticosteroids in Cycle 1, the reviewer agrees that since this action was taken for safety
reasons and given the prevalence and severity of infusion reactions, corticosteroid
administration prior to naxitamab should be advised for all patients in Cycle 1 in the product
label, as well as in the circumstances outlined in the study protocol.
In Trial 201, there were 101 Grade 3 - 4 AEs in the grouped term analysis where IRR. For 31
events, there was no action recorded as being taken with respect to naxitamab, including Grade
3 hypotension (6 patients), bronchospasm, hypoxia, stridor, and respiratory tract edema.
However, there were 98 events of Grade 3 or greater AES which did have an action taken,
including 60 events of hypotension in 14 patients. Study drug was interrupted in 58/60 events
of hypotension which had an action taken with naxitamab.
Reviewer note: See discussion of management of hypotension below. This reviewer does not
agree with the sponsor’s approach to management of hypotension.

Hypotension
Hypotension is included in the pooled term of infusion-related reactions above. FDA notes that
the incidence of Grade ≥3 hypotension was 7% in Trial 12-230 among patients who received the
RP2D, with 11% of patients experiencing an SAE of hypotension. In Trial 201, 60% of patients
experienced Grade ≥3 hypotension. This may be due to the subjectivity of grading hypotension
in CTCAE v4 as described below:
• Grade 2: non-urgent medical intervention indicated
• Grade 3: medical intervention or hospitalization indicated
• Grade 4: life-threatening and urgent intervention indicated.
The toxicity Grade assigned depends upon the healthcare provider’s definition of “non-urgent;”
this reviewer would argue that infusion-associated hypotension would typically prompt urgent
medical intervention (such as stopping or slowing an infusion or providing fluid support if
needed). Therefore the difference in incidence of Grade ≥3 hypotension between the two
primary studies may not represent a true difference in incidence but rather differing
interpretation of the grading system.
As indicated in Section 11,
FDA revised the product label to include a Warning for serious infusion-related
reactions, which includes hypotension.

(b) (4)

The protocol for Trial 201 included instructions for management of hypotension. Infusion
interruption or infusion rate reduction was not required for Grade 2 hypotension. Instructions
are summarized below.
- Grade 3 hypotension without other symptoms:
o Decrease in the rate of naxitamab by 50%; if patient stabilizes, consider resuming
to normal infusion speed based on clinical judgement
- Grade 3 hypotension with other symptoms such as bradycardia, hypoxia, ‘not adequately

Anaphylaxis and Angioedema
Three patients on Trial 201 (12%) and two patients enrolled on Trial 12-203 at any dose (n=117)
experienced serious events of anaphylaxis. One event in Trial 201 was attributed to GM-CSF. A
challenge in interpretation of infusion related reactions is that infusion reactions with
monoclonal antibodies may overlap with anaphylaxis and may be indistinguishable. (LaCasce,
2020)
The majority of events of anaphylaxis (excluding the event attributed to GM-CSF) resulted in
discontinuation of naxitamab. However, in response to an event of Grade 3 anaphylaxis in

dose of naxitamab during cycle 2.

Hypertension
FDA agrees with the description of the incidence of hypertension above. In Trial 12-230, there
were 12 SAEs of hypertension (10.2%), and four led to discontinuation of naxitamab. In
addition, two of these (which led to discontinuation) had MRI findings potentially consistent
with PRES, as described below. Among patients who received the RP2D of naxitamab (n=72), 10
(14%) had an SAE of hypertension.
This reviewer notes that there are limitations to the CTCAE v 4.0 grading of hypertension for
children; CTCAE v 5.0 has revised the definition for pediatric patients. Normal blood pressure
for children varies and norms are classified based on gender, height percentile, and age. CTCAE
v 4.0 provides the following guidance for grading hypertension in children:
• Grade 1: No specific mention of children; for adults, prehypertension (systolic BP 120 ­
139 mm Hg or diastolic BP 80 - 89 mm Hg)
• Grade 2: Recurrent or persistent (>=24 hrs) BP >ULN; monotherapy indicated

•

Grade 3: Stage 2 hypertension (systolic BP >=160 mm Hg or diastolic BP >=100 mm Hg);
medical intervention indicated; more than one drug or more intensive therapy than
previously used indicated. Pediatric: same as adult.
Grade 4: Life-threatening consequences (e.g., malignant hypertension, transient or
permanent neurologic deficit, hypertensive crisis); urgent intervention indicated.
Pediatric: Same as adult.

In practice, for children < 13 years of age, Stage 2 hypertension is classified as systolic and
diastolic blood pressures ≥95th percentiles + 12mm Hg, or ≥ 140/90mm Hg (whichever is
lower), and for children 13 years and older, this is defined as ≥ 140/90mm Hg.
Therefore this reviewer considers that the severity of some events captured as "Grade 2" in
patients who had systolic blood pressures between 140 - 160 mm Hg underestimates the
severity of hypertension when considering the patient's age-based norms. This should be
considered when interpreting the data describing this toxicity.
Two events of hypertension not reported as PRES in Trial 12-230 had MRI findings potentially
consistent with PRES, as described in the narratives below:
Patient
: This is a 3 year old with stage 4 neuroblastoma who experienced
Grade 2 hypertension and Grade 1 urinary retention during his first cycle of naxitamab
(3.0 mg/kg/dose). He received naxitamab as an inpatient for social reasons. He had
intermittent hypertension starting after his first dose (145/96) which continued past the
administration of the third dose of naxitamab. An MRI done two days after the
administration of the last dose of naxitamab showed 'diffuse white matter lesions that
could be suggestive of PRES.' He was discharged 10 days following completion of
naxitamab. Naxitamab was discontinued because of the event.
(b) (6)

Patient
: This is a three year old boy originally diagnosed with Stage 3
neuroblastoma who experienced Grade 3 hypertension the day following infusion of
naxitamab (3.0 mg/kg/dose). He was evaluated in urgent care for a routine blood
pressure check and had a BP of 130/88 while asleep (99th percentile for age 112/71). He
had been experiencing nausea at home but had no neurologic changes concerning for
PRES. He was admitted for blood pressure and nausea control and was discharged the

worsened hypertension (199/78). The family was given a prescription for enalapril to be
given as needed. During his second cycle of naxitamab, he experienced a serious AE of
fever and hypertension. One day after his third dose of naxitamab he was admitted due
to persistent fever and was hypertensive on exam. MRI showed suspicion for subtle
early PRES. Naxitamab was discontinued due to the event.

Neurotoxicity including Pain, Neuropathy and Neurological Disorders of the Eye
Pain
Pain was present in nearly all patients in the safety populations of both Trials 201 and 12-230.
In Trial 201, 100% of patients had any Grade of pain and 72% of patients had Grade ≥ 3 pain.
Events of pain lasted 0 – 28 days with a median of 0 days (indicating the event resolved the
same day). There were 345 events in total, and all but 4 occurred on the day of an infusion of
naxitamab. Among events that had a recorded start time in Trial 201, the median time to onset
was 0.18 hours or approximately 11 minutes (range 0 to 22 hours) following initiation of the
infusion; for Grade 3 events the median time to onset was also 0.18 hours or 11 minutes (range
0.05 hours to 1 hour). Events of pain lasted a median of 2.3 hours (range 0.08 to 249 hours or
10 days). Among Grade 3 events, the median duration was 2.2 hours (range 0.08 to 60 hours or
2.5 days).

hemorrhage and could be related to neurologic disorders of the eye and included the terms
photophobia, pupils unequal, vision blurred, accommodation disorder, optic nerve disorder,
mydriasis, and visual impairment.

Human Reproduction and Pregnancy
The Applicant’s Position:
Pregnancy
No studies of naxitamab in pregnant women or dedicated reproductive toxicity studies in
animals have been performed.
Lactation
No studies have been performed to evaluate the risk potential of naxitamab in lactating and
breastfeeding patients.
Females and Males of Reproductive Potential
In animal toxicity studies, treatment with naxitamab did not induce any morphological changes
in male and female reproductive organs. The potential for treatment with naxitamab to impair
reproductive potential has not been studied in humans.
The FDA’s Assessment:
Refer to section 5.5.4 for a discussion of animal toxicity studies. FDA does not have additional
comments.
Pediatrics and Assessment of Effects on Growth
The Applicant’s Position:
The vast majority of patients for the indicated use of naxitamab are pediatric. The safety and
effectiveness of naxitamab have been established in pediatric patients with high-risk
neuroblastoma based on results from clinical trials in patients aged 12 months and older.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The effect of naxitamab on growth in pediatric patients has not been characterized in this
application.

management. The safety experience with this product represented in this application is largely

reactions in a multicenter setting will be requested from the ongoing multicenter trial (201) as a
postmarketing requirement.

Integrated Assessment of Safety
The Applicant’s Position:
The assessment of safety is supported by data from four clinical trials and data collected for
patients treated under compassionate use.
Common treatment-related events were mainly Grades 1 or 2 and included pain, abdominal
pain, pain in extremity, pyrexia, urticaria, pruritus, nausea, vomiting, hypotension,
hypertension, and bronchospasm. More than 75% of treatment-related events were infusion
related.
Grade 3+ treatment-related events of importance included anaphylaxis, angioedema,
hypotension, bronchospasm, and hypertension. The incidence of treatment-related SAEs across
trials was comparable and below 30%. No deaths occurred.
On the basis of its mechanism of action, naxitamab may cause severe pain, which is expected to
occur for many patients receiving naxitamab. Pain was the most frequently reported adverse
reaction, reported by almost all subjects. These events were associated with naxitamab infusion
and resolved quickly after the infusion was complete. In the naxitamab clinical program, pain
was managed by premedication. Gabapentin was administered at home starting 3 days before
the day of naxitamab infusion. On the day of infusion, opioids were given before and during
infusion to manage pain. Generally, the highest scores for pain were reported during the 30­
minute naxitamab infusion, with pain subsiding before hospital discharge.
Infusion reactions
Naxitamab has been shown to cause infusion reactions, including anaphylaxis, bronchospasm,
skin reactions, oedema, and hypotension. Infusion reactions generally occurred soon after
initiating naxitamab and may last for up to 2 hours after the infusion. Infusion reactions are
most frequent during first infusion in each cycle. The onset of hypotension and bronchospasm
generally occurred during naxitamab infusion. Hypotension and bronchospasm tend to be more
frequent and more severe at the first infusion of a cycle and worst at the first infusion of the
first cycle. In the clinical development program, subjects were premedicated with
antihistamines, antipyretics, and corticosteroids to minimize these risks. Furthermore,
mitigations were in place, including reduction of infusion speed and ability to give concomitant
medications, such as nebulizer with beta2-adrenegic receptor agonist; S2 inhalation; and
additional antihistamines, antipyretics, and/or corticosteroids.
230
Version date: July 24, 2019 (ALL NDA/BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant
and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 4707829

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761171}
{DANYELZA, naxitamab-gqgk}

Anaphylaxis can be a serious reaction to any monoclonal antibody therapy. To mitigate the risk
of anaphylaxis, administration of naxitamab will be contraindicated for any patient that has had
an anaphylactic reaction (CTCAE Grade 4) to another anti-GD2 therapeutic. Part of the
premedication regimen for naxitamab is the administration of corticosteroids and
antihistamines to aid in the prevention of severe immune reactions, and guidance is provided
to physicians to aid in the management of anaphylactic reactions in the product information.
Naxitamab can be administered in an out-patient setting, but must be administered in a facility
by a health care professional with immediate access to emergency equipment and appropriate
medical support to manage severe adverse reactions, if they occur.
Hypertension
Naxitamab may cause severe hypertension. Not all hypertension started on a day of a
naxitamab infusion, but occurred later. For treatment-related hypertension occurring at the day
of naxitamab infusion, 12.0% of SAF subjects reported infusion-related hypertension. In Trial
12-230, about half of the serious hypertensions started on the day of or 1 calendar day after
Cycle 1, Infusion 3, and other serious hypertension events started at earlier infusions in Cycle 1.
PRES was observed in two subjects, but only one subject discontinued treatment. Mitigations
(b) (4)
for hypertension are defined in the label, including additional BP measurements,
Subpopulations
No safety concerns were raised based on subgroup analyses. Safety data demonstrated
patterns of events consistent with those reported for the overall population.
Laboratory findings
In the naxitamab clinical development program, there were no signs of nephrotoxicity,
drug-induced liver injury, or myelosuppression.
In conclusion, the safety profile of naxitamab was well characterized in the clinical development
program of naxitamab in combination with GM-CSF for patients with high-risk refractory or
relapsed neuroblastoma in bone and/or bone marrow.
Events of pain, hypotension, bronchospasm, and hypertension (including CTCAE Grade 3 and
above) are expected to occur for many patients receiving naxitamab, based on the clinical data.
To minimize or mitigate these events, guidance for the management of these is provided in the
label.

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761171}

Naxitamab treatment has proved tolerable for the majority of subjects as shown by the
relatively small number of withdrawals because of adverse events. Most adverse reactions are
limited to the day of infusion, and the effects of the treatment have been manageable through
premedication regimens, analgesic administration during infusions, and careful monitoring of
patients during and in the period after infusion.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The safety of naxitamab in combination with GM-CSF was evaluated in a subpopulation of
patients with high-risk neuroblastoma in bone or bone marrow and had relapsed or refractory
disease, or were in complete remission, from two open-label, single arm studies, Study 201
(n=25) and Study 12-230 (n=72). In both studies, patients received naxitamab as three separate
intravenous infusions of 3 mg/kg (day 1,3 and 5) in first week of a pre-planned 4-week cycle.
Patients also received GM-CSF subcutaneously at 250 µg/m2/day on days -4 to 0 and at 500
µg/m2/day on Days 1 to 5.
During Trials 201 and 12-230 as of the data cut-off for the application, there were no deaths.
Cumulative safety data indicated that SAEs occurred in 32% of patients in Trial 201 and 40% of
patients in Trial 12-230. Serious adverse reactions in > 5% of patients in Trial 201 included
anaphylactic reaction (12%) and pain (8%); in Trial 12-230 SAEs in >5% of patients included
hypertension (14%), hypotension (11%), and pyrexia (8%).
Naxitamab can cause serious infusion-related reactions and neurologic toxicity. Given the
potential severity of these adverse reactions and the occurrence of infusion related reactions
and pain in most patients in either trial, a boxed warning was included in the product label.
Serious infusion reactions required urgent intervention including fluid resuscitation,
administration of bronchodilators and corticosteroids, intensive care unit admission, infusion
rate reduction or interruption of naxitamab infusion. Components of these infusion reactions
included hypotension, bronchospasm, hypoxia, wheezing, and stridor. Infusion reactions of any
Grade occurred in 100% of patients in Trial 201 and 94% of patients in Trial 12-230. In Study
201, 68% of patients in Trial 201 experienced a Grade 3 infusion reaction; while in Study 12­
230, 29% of patients experienced a Grade 3 infusion reaction and 1.4% experienced a Grade 4
infusion reaction. One patient in Study 12-230 (1.4%) experienced a cardiac arrest 1.5 hours
following completion of naxitamab infusion. Anaphylaxis occurred in 12% of patients in Study
201. The product label contains detailed instructions for pre-medications as well as instructions
for slowing, interrupting or permanently discontinuing naxitamab infusions based on the
severity of the adverse reaction.

and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Neurotoxicity experienced in patients receiving naxitamab included pain, peripheral
neuropathy, neurological disorders of the eye, transverse myelitis, and PRES/RPLS. Pain,
including abdominal pain, bone pain, neck pain, and extremity pain, occurred in 100% of
patients in Study 201 and 94% of patients in Study 12-230. Grade 3 pain occurred in 72% of
patients in Study 201.
Peripheral neuropathy including peripheral sensory neuropathy, peripheral motor neuropathy,
paresthesia, and neuralgia occurred in 32% of patients in Trial 201 and in 24% of patients in
Trial 12-230. Neurological disorders of the eye occurred in 19% of patients in Trial 12-230 and
24% of patients in Trial 201; 8% of patients in Study 201 experienced an event that had not
resolved based on the data cut-off of the 120-day safety update. Urinary retention occurred in
4% of patients in Trial 201 and Trial 12-230 and in some patients persisted beyond
discontinuation of opioids. Transverse myelitis occurred in a patient receiving naxitamab
outside of a clinical study. RPLS occurred in 2 (2.8%) patients in Trial 12-230. The product label
describes neurotoxicity as a warning, and provides guidance on dose modifications or
discontinuations
Hypertension occurred in 44% of patients in Study 201 and 28% of patients in Study 12-230 who
received naxitamab. Grade 3 hypertension occurred in 4% of patients in Trial 201 and 7% of
patients in Trial 12-230 and led to permanent discontinuation of naxitamab in some patients. In
both studies, most events occurred on the day of a naxitamab infusion and occurred up to 9
days from an infusion. The product label describes this risk and the need for extended
monitoring of blood pressure.
The most common adverse reactions in Trial 201 (≥25%) were pain, infusion-related reaction,
tachycardia, cough, vomiting, diarrhea, nausea, hypertension, peripheral neuropathy, urticaria,
pyrexia, headache, edema, sinus tachycardia, and fatigue. The most common grade 3 or 4
laboratory abnormalities (≥5%) were decreased neutrophils, decreased lymphocytes, decreased
platelet count, increased alanine aminotransferase and decreased potassium.
The most common adverse reactions (≥25%) in Trial 12-230 were infusion-related reaction,
pain, vomiting, cough, nausea, diarrhea, decreased appetite, sinus tachycardia, fatigue,
erythema multiforme, hypertension, injection site reaction, anxiety, irritability, and localized
edema. The most common grade 3 or 4 laboratory abnormalities (≥5%) were decreased
lymphocytes, decreased neutrophils, decreased hemoglobin, decreased platelet count,
decreased potassium, increased alanine aminotransferase, decreased glucose, decreased
sodium, decreased albumin, and decreased phosphate.

Reference ID: 4707829

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761171}
{DANYELZA, naxitamab-gqgk}

Clinically relevant adverse reactions in ≤10% of patients who received naxitamab with GM-CSF
in either study included apnea, hypopnea, generalized edema, peripheral edema, and device
related infection.. Events of apnea, hypopnea, unresponsiveness and somnolence also occurred
in some patients and may be related to concomitant administration of opioids or to cooccurring events of severe infusion reactions, such as severe hypotension.
The safety population included patients ages 1 to 24 years of age across both trials, with a
median age of 5 years in both trials. Although relatively few young adult patients and very
young patients (2 years of age or younger) were enrolled, exploratory analyses of safety trends
did not demonstrate a significant difference in safety across groups.
Overall, the risks associated with naxitamab appear acceptable in the setting of patients with
relapsed or refractory neuroblastoma who have no approved therapies. Naxitamab is expected
to be administered in infusion centers with intensive monitoring and the ability to quickly
respond to events such as severe infusion reactions. In addition, the existence of previously
approved products in the US (dinutuximab) with a similar mechanism of action and toxicity
profile means that the majority of pediatric oncologists administering naxitamab will have
familiarity with expected toxicities associated with anti-GD2 antibodies. FDA assessed that the
communication of risk in the product label required the addition of a boxed warning given the
potential severity of the reactions.
